## ERA 9 — 1950 to 2000 (`state="full"`)

### 9a. [VERIFY] CLEARED — the 1962 and 1963 cases, told from the faith side

**The two cases are *Engel v. Vitale*, decided 25 June 1962, and *School District of Abington Township
v. Schempp*, decided 17 June 1963** (argued with *Murray v. Curlett* from Baltimore). Both opinions were
read in full from the scanned U.S. Reports:
https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep370/usrep370421/usrep370421.pdf and
https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep374/usrep374203/usrep374203.pdf

**WHAT THE OTHER CHAPTERS ALREADY OWN — do not repeat it.** `outlines/education.md:782-790` gives both
rulings, the 22-word Regents prayer in full, Justice Black's line about government composing prayers,
Pennsylvania's ten-verse statute, Justice Clark's "strict neutrality," and the vote counts.
`outlines/government-politics.md:338` gives the same again as constitutional law. **This chapter must not
quote the Regents prayer, Black's line, or Clark's neutrality line.** What is left, and what is genuinely
this chapter's, is **whose faith was at stake on each side.**

**The families in *Engel* were religious people, and they were not one religion.** The parents of **ten
pupils** sued, saying the official prayer "was contrary to the beliefs, religions, or religious practices
of both themselves and their children." Justice Douglas, concurring, recorded what the New York court had
found: the prayer "does not conform to all of the tenets of the Jewish, Unitarian, and Ethical Culture
groups," and **"One of the petitioners is an agnostic."** (370 U.S. 421, at 423 and 439.)

**The Schempps were Unitarians, and they went to church.** Edward Lewis Schempp, his wife Sidney and
their children Roger and Donna "are of the Unitarian faith and are members of the Unitarian Church in
Germantown, Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, where they, as well as another son, Ellory, regularly attend
religious services." Ellory had started the case and left it when he graduated. (374 U.S. 203, at 205-206.)

**Why Edward Schempp would not use the excusal, in the trial court's summary of his testimony — this is
the strongest single passage in the record for this chapter's angle.** He decided against having Roger or
Donna excused because he thought they would be "labeled as 'odd balls'" by teachers and classmates every
school day; because classmates were liable "to lump all particular religious difference[s] or religious
objections [together] as 'atheism'"; because in 1962 "atheism" was connected with "atheistic communism"
and carried "very bad" meanings like "un-American"; because the exercises ran straight into the school
announcements, so a child who left would miss them; and because an excused child "would have to stand in
the hall outside their 'homeroom' and that this carried with it the imputation of punishment for bad
conduct." (374 U.S. 203, at 208 n. 3, quoting 201 F. Supp., at 818.)

**Two expert witnesses, one on each side, and what they said about the Bible.**
- **Dr. Solomon Grayzel**, for the families: the most obvious difference between the Jewish scriptures and
  the Christian Bible is that the Jewish scriptures have no New Testament; from the standpoint of Jewish
  faith the idea of Jesus as the Son of God is "practically blasphemous"; parts of the New Testament
  "tended to bring the Jews into ridicule or scorn"; read without explanation those parts had, in his own
  experience with children, been "psychologically harmful to the child."
- **Dr. Luther A. Weigle**, for the school district, said on direct examination "that the Bible was
  non-sectarian." **"He later stated that the phrase 'non-sectarian' meant to him non-sectarian within
  the Christian faiths."** (374 U.S. 203, at 209-210.)
- **That exchange is the teaching point of the whole span** and it is in the Court's own record.

**The Murray case, and what atheism meant in 1963.** Baltimore's rule, dating from 1905, opened each
school with "reading, without comment, of a chapter in the Holy Bible and/or the use of the Lord's
Prayer," and let pupils use the Douay version if they preferred. **Mrs. Madalyn Murray and her son
William J. Murray III "are both professed atheists."** They got the rule amended so a child could be
excused, William was excused, and they carried on with the case anyway, arguing the rule "threatens their
religious liberty by placing a premium on belief as against non-belief." (374 U.S. 203, at 211-212.)

**[VERIFY] CLEARED — "religion taught *about* rather than practised" is the Court's own carve-out, in
*Schempp*.** Justice Clark: "it might well be said that one's education is not complete without a study
of comparative religion or the history of religion and its relationship to the advancement of
civilization. It certainly may be said that the Bible is worthy of study for its literary and historic
qualities. Nothing we have said here indicates that such study of the Bible or of religion, when
presented objectively as part of a secular program of education, may not be effected consistently with
the First Amendment." (374 U.S. 203, at 225.)

### 9b. What people said the day after — from the newspaper of that day, not from memory

**Source: *Evening Star* (Washington, D.C.), 26 June 1962, page 1**, Library of Congress Chronicling
America, http://www.loc.gov/resource/sn83045462/1962-06-26/ed-1/?sp=1 . Two stories on that front page.

- **"Court Action Stirs Storm Of Criticism" (Associated Press).** A constitutional amendment to get
  around the ruling **was introduced in the House the very next day**, and more were being drafted. The
  paper describes "the cauldron of criticism stirred up among churchmen and legislators."
  - **Representative Taylor, Democrat of North Carolina, was first** — the paper notes that he "lists
    Billy Graham among his constituents."
  - **Senator Beall, Republican of Maryland**, had already announced one for the Senate.
  - **Representative Chelf, Democrat of Kentucky**, was sounding out the House Judiciary Committee, and
    said that if he could not get legislation he would favour **limiting Supreme Court justices to a set
    number of years instead of appointment for life.**
  - **Representative Johnson, Democrat of Maryland**, was drafting an amendment to permit "non compulsory
    prayers in public schools and other places."
- **"Area Officials Unsure Of Prayer Ban Effects."** The District of Columbia Board of Education required
  school-day opening exercises to include "a reading from the Bible, without note or comment, and the
  Lord's Prayer" — **the only such rule among the seven school systems in the Washington area.**
  Superintendent **Carl F. Hansen** said that "on the surface it appears that it does invalidate the
  board's rule," and that he would ask the Corporation Counsel to be sure.
- **Use this instead of a general sentence about people being upset.** It is dated, named and checkable,
  and it shows the fight moving to Congress within twenty-four hours.

### 9c. [VERIFY] CLEARED — what schools did instead: released time, a moment of silence, student-led prayer

**All four items in the workspace's [VERIFY] line are settled below, each from the Supreme Court's own
report.**

- **RELEASED TIME — two cases, four years apart, opposite results.**
  - ***Illinois ex rel. McCollum v. Board of Education*, decided 8 March 1948** (era 8 by date). In
    Champaign, Illinois, a private group called the **Champaign Council on Religious Education**,
    including Catholic, Protestant and Jewish teachers, came into the public school buildings **once a
    week** and taught religion for **thirty minutes in the lower grades and forty-five in the higher**.
    Children whose parents asked were excused from their ordinary classes and **required to attend the
    religious classes**; the rest stayed in their classrooms, because school attendance was compulsory.
    **Vashti McCollum**, a resident, taxpayer and the mother of a pupil, sued. The Court held that using
    the public schools' buildings and their compulsory-attendance machinery to let religious groups teach
    religion violated the First Amendment. (333 U.S. 203, syllabus and at 207-212,
    https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep333/usrep333203/usrep333203.pdf )
  - ***Zorach v. Clauson*, decided 28 April 1952.** New York City let pupils **leave the school buildings
    and grounds**, on their parents' written request, and go to religious centres for instruction; the
    churches reported back to the schools the names of released children who failed to show up. **The
    Court upheld it**, distinguishing *McCollum*, because the programme "involves neither religious
    instruction in public schools nor the expenditure of public funds." (343 U.S. 306, syllabus,
    https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep343/usrep343306/usrep343306.pdf )
  - **The line the Court drew is a physical one: religion taught inside the school building is out;
    children released to go elsewhere is in.** That is a plain thing to explain to a child.
- **A MOMENT OF SILENCE — *Wallace v. Jaffree*, decided 4 June 1985.** Alabama passed three laws in a
  row: **section 16-1-20 in 1978**, a one-minute silence "for meditation"; **section 16-1-20.1 in 1981**,
  a silence "for meditation or voluntary prayer"; and **section 16-1-20.2 in 1982**, which let teachers
  lead "willing students" in a set prayer to "Almighty God . . . the Creator and Supreme Judge of the
  world." The Court struck down the 1981 law because its purpose was to endorse religion. **It did not
  strike down the 1978 law providing plain silence for meditation.** (472 U.S. 38, syllabus and at 40,
  59-61, https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep472/usrep472038/usrep472038.pdf )
- **CLERGY-LED PRAYER AT A SCHOOL CEREMONY — *Lee v. Weisman*, decided 24 June 1992.** Principal Robert
  E. Lee of **Nathan Bishop Middle School in Providence, Rhode Island**, invited **Rabbi Leslie Gutterman
  of Temple Beth El** to give the invocation and benediction at **Deborah Weisman's** graduation. The
  school handed the rabbi a pamphlet, **"Guidelines for Civic Occasions," prepared by the National
  Conference of Christians and Jews**, and told him the prayers should be nonsectarian. The invocation
  began "God of the Free, Hope of the Brave." Deborah's father **Daniel Weisman** sued. The Court held
  the practice unconstitutional, saying that by giving the rabbi the pamphlet and the instruction the
  principal "directed and controlled the content of the prayers." (505 U.S. 577, at 581-582, 588,
  https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep505/usrep505577/usrep505577.pdf )
- **PARKED FOR THE ERA-10 AGENT, because of the boundary rule.** ***Santa Fe Independent School District
  v. Doe* was decided 19 June 2000**, so by the project's boundary rule it belongs in `2000-today`, not
  here. Facts, ready to use: before 1995 a student elected as **Santa Fe High School's student council
  chaplain** said a prayer over the public address system before every home varsity football game; the
  district then set up **two student elections**, one on whether to have "invocations" at games and one to
  pick who would give them. The families who sued were **one Mormon and one Catholic**. The Court struck
  the policy down. (530 U.S. 290, syllabus and at 294, 305,
  https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep530/usrep530290/usrep530290.pdf )
  **That case is also the natural pairing for the era-10 [VERIFY] about a coach praying at a school
  game (*Kennedy v. Bremerton School District*, 2022).**

### 9d. [VERIFY] CLEARED — the 1968 and 1987 evolution cases, told from the faith side

**The two cases are *Epperson v. Arkansas*, 393 U.S. 97, argued 16 October 1968 and decided 12 November
1968, and *Edwards v. Aguillard*, 482 U.S. 578, argued 10 December 1986 and decided 19 June 1987.** Both
opinions were read in full from the scanned U.S. Reports:
https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep393/usrep393097/usrep393097.pdf and
https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep482/usrep482578/usrep482578.pdf

**WHAT THE OTHER CHAPTERS OWN.** `science` and `education` carry evolution as a subject and as
curriculum; `government-politics` carries the establishment-clause rule. **This chapter's slice is the
religious argument in each case, and in both the Court said out loud whose religion it was about.**

**EPPERSON — the facts, from the opinion.**
- **Susan Epperson** graduated from Arkansas's own school system, took a master's degree in zoology at
  the University of Illinois, and was hired by the Little Rock school system **in the autumn of 1964 to
  teach 10th-grade biology at Central High School**. Until then the official biology textbook had no
  section on Darwin's theory. **For the school year 1965-1966** the school administration, on the
  recommendation of the system's biology teachers, adopted a textbook with a chapter on "the theory about
  the origin . . . of man from a lower form of animal." Teaching that chapter was a crime under the 1928
  Arkansas law, so she sued. (393 U.S. 97, at 99-100.)
- **A parent joined her: "H. H. Blanchard, a parent of children attending the public schools, intervened
  in support of the action."** (393 U.S. 97, at 100.) The Encyclopedia of Arkansas gives the filing date
  as **6 December 1965** in Pulaski County Chancery Court, the trial as beginning **1 April 1966**, and
  Chancellor Murray O. Reed's ruling for Epperson as **27 May 1966**; the Arkansas Supreme Court reversed
  him in 1967 in a two-sentence opinion.
  (CALS Encyclopedia of Arkansas, "Epperson v. Arkansas," staff entry, page last updated 11 June 2026,
  https://encyclopediaofarkansas.net/entries/epperson-v-arkansas-2528/ )
- **The Encyclopedia of Arkansas also records who organised the case:** Forrest Rozzell, executive
  secretary of the Arkansas Education Association, began planning a lawsuit against the state, and
  Epperson became the plaintiff. **Same source.**
- **The Court named the religion.** From the syllabus: "The sole reason for the Arkansas law is that a
  particular religious group considers the evolution theory to conflict with the account of the origin of
  man set forth in the Book of Genesis." In the body: "there can be no doubt that Arkansas has sought to
  prevent its teachers from discussing the theory of evolution because it is contrary to the belief of
  some that the Book of Genesis must be the exclusive source of doctrine as to the origin of man," and
  "fundamentalist sectarian conviction was and is the law's reason for existence." (393 U.S. 97,
  syllabus (b), and at 107-108.)
- **The Court also said the rule cuts both ways:** the First Amendment "mandates governmental neutrality
  between religion and religion, and between religion and nonreligion." (Syllabus (c).)
- **A caution about the vote.** The Encyclopedia of Arkansas says the Court ruled unanimously. The
  printed opinion shows Justice Fortas writing for the Court with **separate opinions by Justices Black,
  Harlan and Stewart**, and Justice Black disagreeing in plain terms with the Court's reasoning: "I find
  it difficult to agree with the Court's statement that 'there can be no doubt that Arkansas has sought
  to prevent its teachers from discussing the theory of evolution . . .'" **Say the Court struck the law
  down with no justice voting to uphold it, and do not say the justices agreed on why.**

**EDWARDS v. AGUILLARD — the facts, from the opinion.**
- The Louisiana law at issue was the **"Balanced Treatment for Creation-Science and Evolution-Science in
  Public School Instruction" Act**, and it "forbids the teaching of the theory of evolution in public
  elementary and secondary schools unless accompanied by instruction in the theory of 'creation
  science.'" Neither had to be taught; but neither could be taught alone. (482 U.S. 578, syllabus.)
- **Who sued is this chapter's point.** The people who brought the case "include parents of children
  attending Louisiana public schools, Louisiana teachers, and religious leaders." (482 U.S. 578, at
  581.) **Religious people were on both sides of this case, and the record shows it on its face.**
- **The friend-of-the-court briefs, listed in the opinion's own footnote, are the clearest evidence of
  that split.** Urging that the law be upheld: the **Catholic League for Religious and Civil Rights**,
  the **Christian Legal Society**, and **Concerned Women for America**. Urging that it be struck down:
  the **New York Committee for Public Education and Religious Liberty**, People for the American Way,
  the American Association of University Professors, the American Federation of Teachers, the State of
  New York and other states, and **72 Nobel Laureates**. Separate briefs came from the **Rabbinical
  Alliance of America** and from **"Reverend Bill McLean et al."** (482 U.S. 578, at 579-580 and n. *.)
- **What the Court held about the law's purpose:** the Act "impermissibly endorses religion by advancing
  the religious belief that a supernatural being created humankind," and "The preeminent purpose of the
  Louisiana Legislature was clearly to advance the religious viewpoint that a supernatural being created
  humankind." (Syllabus 1(b), and at 591.)
- **The vote was 7 to 2.** Justice Brennan wrote the opinion, joined by Marshall, Blackmun, Powell and
  Stevens, and by O'Connor in all but Part II; Powell filed a concurrence joined by O'Connor; White
  concurred in the judgment; **Scalia dissented, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist.** (482 U.S. 578, at
  580.)
- **The bridge between the two cases, in the Court's own words:** "These same historic and
  contemporaneous antagonisms between the teachings of certain religious denominations and the teaching
  of evolution are present in this case." (482 U.S. 578, at 590.)

### 9e. [VERIFY] CLEARED — the county-wide textbook fight of the 1970s: Kanawha County, West Virginia, 1974

**Source: e-WV, The West Virginia Encyclopedia (published by the West Virginia Humanities Council),
"Kanawha County Textbook Controversy," by Shirley A. Smith,
https://www.wvencyclopedia.org/entries/1064 . The entry's own listed sources are the National Education
Association's *Kanawha County, West Virginia: A Textbook Study in Cultural Conflict* (1975), James
Moffett's *Storm in the Mountains* (Southern Illinois University Press, 1989), Franklin Parker's *The
Battle of the Books: Kanawha County* (Phi Delta Kappa, 1975), and Carol Mason in *Appalachian Journal*
32:3 (Spring 2005).**

- **The county had 46,000 pupils in 124 public schools.**
- **April 1974:** the five-member Kanawha County Board of Education voted unanimously, after a public
  review, to adopt **325 language-arts textbooks and supplementary books**.
- **The following month** board member **Alice Moore** challenged the content and the philosophy of some
  of them. She had been elected to the board **in 1970** campaigning against sex education, and she was
  **the wife of a fundamentalist minister**. (The e-WV entry's words. A second source, below, names the
  denomination.) The purchase was delayed, and she and several ministers campaigned against the books.
- **A minister led the other side too: Jim Lewis, a Charleston minister, was the books' most vocal
  supporter.** This is the fact that keeps the span honest — clergy were on both sides in one county.
- **Petitions carrying 12,000 signatures**, and a condemnation of the books by **27 ministers** on
  grounds of immorality and indecency, did not stop the board: **in June it voted 3 to 2 to accept most
  of the books.**
- **When school opened on 3 September 1974, attendance dropped 20 per cent** because of calls for a
  boycott. Picketing spread from the schools to businesses and to the coal mines, where **3,500 miners
  walked out in a sympathy strike.** The Kanawha Circuit Court issued an injunction on **6 September**
  against interfering with people going in.
- **What the protest turned into, in the encyclopedia's own list: gunfire, firebombs, dynamite attacks on
  schools, and 11 arrests. The schools were closed on 12 September for four days**, and there was
  sporadic violence through November.
- **It did not end in 1974.** The board set up committees that put parents into textbook selection and
  agreed to modified guidelines barring certain content. Protests ran on **into 1977**, and drew in the
  John Birch Society and the Ku Klux Klan. **The Reverend Marvin Horan was tried for plotting to bomb
  schools**, and that trial marked the end of the violence.
- **NOT SETTLED, and left out rather than hedged:** no accessible source gives a count of people injured
  by the gunfire or the firebombs, or says whether anyone was killed. **Do not write a casualty figure.**
- **Alice Moore's denomination and her death, from a second source, which is journalism and is labelled
  as such:** Alice Whitehurst Moore died **9 September 2023 at the age of 82**, at Acton, Tennessee; she
  was **"the wife of a Church of Christ preacher"** and had five children. (Trey Kay, West Virginia
  Public Broadcasting, 11 September 2023,
  https://wvpublic.org/us-them-host-trey-kay-remembers-alice-moore/ ) **The e-WV entry says
  "fundamentalist minister" and this one says "Church of Christ preacher." Both are given; neither is
  resolved into the other.**
- **NO STORY BLOCK for Alice Moore.** No source reached from this environment carries her own words about
  the books at length. She is named in the span for what she did, which the record settles, and nothing
  is said about why she did it beyond what she herself campaigned on.

### 9f. [VERIFY] CLEARED — the 1972 Amish ruling, and the question Justice Douglas asked

***Wisconsin v. Yoder*, 406 U.S. 205, argued 8 December 1971 and decided 15 May 1972.** Read in full from
https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep406/usrep406205/usrep406205.pdf

**WHAT THE OTHER CHAPTERS OWN — do not repeat it.** `outlines/education.md` gives the three fathers, the
three children and their ages, the $5 fines, Wisconsin's age-16 rule and Chief Justice Burger's holding.
`outlines/government-politics.md:341` gives the same again as law. **This chapter must not restate the
fines or the holding.** What is left is the belief itself, and the argument about whose belief it was.

- **The families and their churches.** Jonas Yoder and Wallace Miller were **Old Order Amish**; Adin
  Yutzy belonged to the **Conservative Amish Mennonite Church**. All lived in **Green County,
  Wisconsin.** (406 U.S. 205, at 207.)
- **The verse.** The Court traced the objection to one line of scripture: the Amish way of life "is not
  merely a matter of personal preference, but one of deep religious conviction, shared by an organized
  group, and intimately related to daily living," and it comes "in response to their literal
  interpretation of the Biblical injunction from the Epistle of Paul to the Romans, 'be not conformed to
  this world . . . .' This command is fundamental to the Amish faith." (406 U.S. 205, at 216.)
- **What the objection actually was.** They did not object to the first eight grades, because children
  need the basic skills "in order to read the Bible, to be good farmers and citizens, and to be able to
  deal with non-Amish people." They objected to high school, which they saw as "an impermissible exposure
  of their children to a 'worldly' influence." (406 U.S. 205, at 210-211.)
- **The expert witness.** **Dr. John Hostetler**, an authority on Amish society, testified that
  compulsory high school could cause great psychological harm to Amish children and would in his opinion
  end in "the destruction of the Old Order Amish church community as it exists in the United States
  today." (406 U.S. 205, at 212.)
- **THE PART NO OTHER CHAPTER HAS, AND THE REASON THIS BELONGS TO RELIGION. One of the children
  testified, and Justice Douglas dissented in part over the other two.** The Court's own footnote 21:
  "The only relevant testimony in the record is to the effect that the wishes of the one child who
  testified corresponded with those of her parents. Testimony of Frieda Yoder, Tr. 92-94, to the effect
  that her personal religious beliefs guided her decision to discontinue school attendance after the
  eighth grade. The other children were not called by either side." Justice Douglas: "Frieda Yoder has in
  fact testified that her own religious views are opposed to high-school education. I therefore join the
  judgment of the Court as to respondent Jonas Yoder. But Frieda Yoder's views may not be those of Vernon
  Yutzy or Barbara Miller. I must dissent, therefore, as to respondents Adin Yutzy and Wallace Miller as
  their motion to dismiss also raised the question of their children's religious liberty." (406 U.S. 205,
  at 237 n. 21, and at 241-242, Douglas, J., dissenting in part.)
- **Douglas's question in plain words, for the writer: when a family's religion decides a child's
  schooling, is it the parents' religion or the child's? He said the Court had never squarely faced it.**

### 9g. STORY — Jonas and Frieda Yoder (`jonas-and-frieda-yoder`) — the `curriculum-objector-parent` TARGET SLOT IS FILLED, AND REPLACED

**The slot as written asked for "a parent who objected to or defended a school's teaching."** It is filled
by a father and a daughter whose religious reasons are both on the record in a primary source, which is
what the slot was for. **Every fact below is from the Supreme Court's own report of the case, cited in
§9f.** No composite; both people are real and named in the opinion.

- **Jonas Yoder**, of Green County, Wisconsin, a member of the Old Order Amish. His daughter **Frieda
  Yoder** was **15** and had finished the eighth grade of the public school.
- Wisconsin required school until 16. He did not send her, because Amish belief takes the line in Paul's
  letter to the Romans, "be not conformed to this world," as a rule for daily life, and treats the high
  school as the world.
- He was charged, tried and convicted with **Wallace Miller** and **Adin Yutzy** in the Green County
  Court.
- **Frieda was the only child in the case who testified.** She said her own religious beliefs were the
  reason she had stopped going to school after the eighth grade.
- The Supreme Court decided for the fathers on 15 May 1972. **Justice Douglas agreed as to Jonas Yoder
  and disagreed as to the other two, because their children had not been asked.**
- **Movie:** none verified. `movie=""`.
- **Shared with:** `education` (the school day and the attendance law) · `government-politics` (the
  ruling as law). Neither carries Frieda's testimony or Douglas's partial dissent.

### 9h. The postwar boom, and the two laws that put God into the country's official words

**This is the era frame, and it is built from statutes and one pollster, both labelled.**

- **"under God" was added to the Pledge of Allegiance by Act of Congress.** Public Law 83-396, a joint
  resolution "To amend the pledge of allegiance to the flag of the United States of America," **approved
  14 June 1954**, rewrote section 7 of the 1942 flag resolution so that the pledge reads: "I pledge
  allegiance to the flag of the United States of America and to the Republic for which it stands, one
  Nation under God, indivisible, with liberty and justice for all."
  (68 Stat. 249, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-68/pdf/STATUTE-68-Pg249.pdf )
- **"In God we trust" was made the national motto two years later.** Public Law 84-851, a joint
  resolution "To establish a national motto of the United States," **approved 30 July 1956**: "That the
  national motto of the United States is hereby declared to be 'In God we trust.'"
  (70 Stat. 732, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-70/pdf/STATUTE-70-Pg732.pdf )
  **OCR WARNING, and the trap the brief names.** The scanned page prints the approval line as "Approved
  July 30, 1966." That is an OCR misread of 1956: the running head, the bill's own date line and the
  next law on the page (Public Law 852, 31 July 1956) all give 1956. **Write 1956.**
- **`money` LEADS on "In God We Trust" as coinage** — `outlines/money.md:148` has the 1864 two-cent
  piece, the Reverend M. R. Watkinson's 1861 letter to Salmon P. Chase, and paper money in 1957. **Do
  not retell any of that.** This chapter's fact is the 1956 motto statute, which is a different thing.
- **Church membership, from a pollster, labelled as one.** Gallup has asked Americans since 1937 whether
  they belong to a church, synagogue or mosque. **73 per cent said yes in 1937; the figure stayed near
  70 per cent from the late 1930s through the 1990s and was 70 per cent in 1999; it was 50 per cent in
  2018 and 47 per cent in 2020.** Rolled into three-year averages: **69 per cent in 1998-2000, 62 per
  cent in 2008-2010, 49 per cent in 2018-2020.** Over the same three periods the share of adults giving
  **no religious preference** went **8 per cent, 13 per cent, 21 per cent.** By generation in 2018-2020:
  **Traditionalists born before 1946, 66 per cent; Baby Boomers, 58; Generation X, 50; Millennials,
  36.** (Jeffrey M. Jones, "U.S. Church Membership Falls Below Majority for First Time," Gallup,
  published **29 March 2021**, from 6,117 interviews taken in 2018-2020,
  https://news.gallup.com/poll/341963/church-membership-falls-below-majority-first-time.aspx )
  **Gallup is a polling company, not a government count, and the book must say so.** The 2020 crossing
  below half is **era-10** material by the boundary rule; the flat 70 per cent through the whole of era 9
  is the era-9 fact.

### 9i. STORY — Billy Graham (`billy-graham`, status upgraded from `candidate` to `verified`)

**Sources, all primary or institutional:** NCpedia, the encyclopedia of the **State Library of North
Carolina**, "Graham, William Franklin Jr.," by Amy Kemp, 2017, revised by the Government & Heritage
Library 2018, https://www.ncpedia.org/graham-william-billy-franklin-jr · **Public Law 104-111** (110
Stat. 772), https://www.congress.gov/104/plaws/publ111/PLAW-104publ111.pdf · the **Martin Luther King, Jr.
Research and Education Institute, Stanford University**, entry "Graham, William Franklin,"
https://kinginstitute.stanford.edu/graham-william-franklin and its document "Invocation Delivered at
Billy Graham Evangelistic Association Crusade,"
https://kinginstitute.stanford.edu/king-papers/documents/invocation-delivered-billy-graham-evangelistic-association-crusade
· **H.Con.Res. 107, 115th Congress**, agreed to 26 February 2018,
https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/BILLS-115hconres107enr/pdf/BILLS-115hconres107enr.pdf

- **Born 7 November 1918 in Charlotte, North Carolina**, to a dairy-farming family. NCpedia records that
  he decided to give his life to God in **1934** after hearing a travelling evangelist preach in
  Charlotte. **Ordained by the Southern Baptists in 1939.** Theology degree from the **Florida Bible
  Institute in 1940**; BA from **Wheaton College, Illinois, in 1943**. Married **Ruth Bell in August
  1943**; five children.
- **1944 to 1948: Youth for Christ**, travelling the country speaking at rallies.
- **1949, Los Angeles.** A campaign booked for **three weeks ran for seven**. NCpedia calls this the point
  at which his speaking became widely known. He then founded the **Billy Graham Evangelistic
  Association**, and a film arm called World Wide Pictures. Weekly radio, *Hour of Decision*, and a daily
  newspaper column, *My Answer*, followed in the early 1950s.
- **1954, London: a twelve-week campaign.** **1957, New York:** the Madison Square Garden crusade ran
  **nightly for sixteen weeks.**
- **THE FACT THIS CHAPTER IS FOR, and it is documented in a university archive, not in a memoir.** The
  King Institute's editorial note: Graham opened the New York crusade at Madison Square Garden on **15
  May 1957** with **eighteen thousand people** at the first meeting, and it ran far past its planned six
  weeks. After a telephone conversation between Graham and **Martin Luther King, Jr.**, the evangelist
  **Leighton Ford** sent King a telegram on **5 July 1957** asking him to give the invocation; King
  accepted three days later. **On 18 July 1957 King gave the opening prayer at Graham's crusade**, asked
  for help "for a warless world and for a brotherhood that transcends race or color," and led the
  audience in the Lord's Prayer. The recording is in the Records of the Billy Graham Evangelistic
  Association, Billy Graham Center Archives, Wheaton College.
- **King wrote to Graham on 31 August 1957: "You have courageously brought the Christian gospel to bear
  on the question of race."** The same King Institute entry records that King **objected in a letter of
  23 July 1958** to Graham sharing a platform with Governor Price Daniel of Texas, calling it an
  "endorsement of racial segregation and discrimination." **Both letters go in. One without the other is
  a half-truth.** The entry also records that Graham held **integrated crusades in Birmingham, Alabama at
  Easter 1964** and toured Alabama in **1965**.
- **What Congress said about him, in a statute — and it is Congress's claim, not this book's.** The
  findings in Public Law 104-111 state that "Billy Graham's crusades have reached 100,000,000 people in
  person and reached over 2,000,000,000 people worldwide on television." **The Speaker of the House and
  the President pro tempore of the Senate were authorised to present Billy and Ruth Graham a gold
  medal.** The Act was **approved 13 February 1996** and the medal was presented **in the Capitol Rotunda
  on 2 May 1996.** **DO NOT repeat the Act's phrase "throughout his 76 years of life": Graham was 77 when
  it was signed. Congress's arithmetic is not a fact about Graham.**
- NCpedia's own figures, which differ from the statute's because they count different things: **"preached
  to over 215 million people in 185 countries."** Give whichever is used with its source attached.
- He **stepped down from the Billy Graham Evangelistic Association in 2000**, held a last crusade in
  **Queens, New York in 2005**, and **died at Montreat, North Carolina on 21 February 2018, aged 99.**
- **On 28 February 2018 his body lay in honour in the Rotunda of the Capitol**, under H.Con.Res. 107,
  agreed to by Congress on 26 February 2018, which authorised the use of the Rotunda from 28 February to
  1 March 2018. **The House's own account says he was the fourth private citizen to lie in honour
  there** (https://www.house.gov/feature-stories/2018-2-27-rev-billy-graham ).
- **Movie:** none verified. `movie=""`. World Wide Pictures made films; no documentary about Graham was
  confirmed from an accessible source, so the field stays empty.
- **NOT SETTLED, and left out rather than asserted.** The often-repeated story that Graham personally
  pulled down the segregation ropes at a 1953 crusade in Chattanooga comes from his own memoir, *Just as
  I Am* (1997), which could not be read from this environment. **It is not in the outline.** The 1957
  King invocation and the two King letters are used instead, because they are documented in a university
  archive.

### 9j. [VERIFY] N/A — the congregation that housed a movement: Bethel Baptist Church, Birmingham

**Source: the National Historic Landmark nomination for "Bethel Baptist Church, Parsonage, and
Guardhouse," prepared by Susan Cianci Salvatore, Preservation Planner, National Park Service, National
Historic Landmarks Survey, April 2004, edited by John H. Sprinkle, Jr., NPS reference 05000455,
https://npgallery.nps.gov/NRHP/GetAsset/NRHP/05000455_text .** Second source: **Encyclopedia of Alabama**,
"Alabama Christian Movement for Human Rights," by Andrew Manis (Macon State College), published 3 June
2010, last updated 20 March 2024,
https://encyclopediaofalabama.org/article/alabama-christian-movement-for-human-rights-acmhr/ . Third:
**National Park Service, "The Historic Bethel Baptist Church,"**
https://www.nps.gov/places/the-historic-bethel-baptist-church.htm

**WHAT `rights-movements` OWNS AND THIS CHAPTER MUST NOT RETELL.** `outlines/rights-movements.md:891`
carries the Freedom Riders and Shuttlesworth's convoy; :895-906 carries the Birmingham children's march,
the hoses and dogs, and the 16th Street Baptist Church bombing with the four girls named; :941 carries
Brown Chapel and the Edmund Pettus Bridge; :998 carries Dexter Avenue Baptist Church and the Montgomery
Improvement Association. **None of that is retold here.** This chapter's angle is a congregation as a
congregation: what a church building, a pastor's house and a deacon's front porch were used for, and
what was done to them.

- **The church was built in 1926** in Collegeville, a Black working-class neighbourhood six miles north
  of the centre of Birmingham. **Fred L. Shuttlesworth was its pastor from March 1953.**
- **Why a church body had to do this work.** An Alabama circuit court **banned the NAACP from operating
  in the state on 1 June 1956.** Shuttlesworth and other ministers drew up a declaration on **2 June**
  and founded the **Alabama Christian Movement for Human Rights at Sardis Baptist Church on 5 June
  1956**, at a meeting of about **a thousand people**. The Encyclopedia of Alabama records his reasoning
  for the name: officials could outlaw an organisation but not a movement, and the word "Christian" both
  set it apart from groups being called Communist and gave it "a foundation and Christian orientation."
  **Bethel Baptist Church was the ACMHR's headquarters from 1956 to 1961.**
- **What the meetings were.** Weekly mass meetings **every Monday night**, led by Shuttlesworth at
  churches around Birmingham, with, in the National Park Service's words, "emotional testimony,
  soul-lifting music, impassioned preaching, and fundraising." The Encyclopedia of Alabama records
  several hundred people at them routinely, and roughly **1,000 members in 1959, 60 per cent of them
  women**, most of them working people. The NHL nomination adds that by 1958 weekly meetings were also
  being held at **55 other working-class churches** in the city.
- **THE FIRST BOMB. On the night of 25 December 1956 — Christmas night — twelve sticks of dynamite
  exploded in the space between the church and the parsonage.** The parsonage was destroyed. Shuttlesworth
  was inside talking with a church deacon and his family was watching television. The blast "shattered
  windows, caved in the roof of the living room and wrecked furnishings," and Shuttlesworth was among the
  injured, with a minor head injury. The bomb was set the night before the ACMHR had announced its
  members would ride the segregated buses; **250 people rode them the next day and 22 were arrested.**
  (NHL nomination, pages 13 and 4; the nomination cites "Bombing in Alabama Injures 5, Wrecks Minister's
  Home," *New York Times*, 26 December 1956, page 1.)
- **THE SECOND BOMB, AND THE GUARDS. At about 1.30 in the morning on Sunday 29 June 1958, Bethel Church
  guards carried a smoking explosive away from the side of the church and put it in the street, where it
  went off**, blowing a hole in the road and out some church windows without damaging the structure of
  the building. Birmingham police went to the house of **James Revis**, a Bethel deacon living across the
  street, and took four rifles from the guards on his porch. The nomination records that after the
  Christmas 1956 bomb the ACMHR guards had been guarding the church with what they called their
  "nonviolent Winchesters." **A third bomb in 1962 shattered the church windows.** The 1958 bombing was
  not solved until **1980**, when **J. B. Stoner** was convicted in *Alabama v. Stoner*.
- **THE GUARDHOUSE — this is the ordinary-people material, and it comes from recorded oral history.**
  After the 1956 bomb destroyed the old parsonage, ACMHR and Bethel church members volunteered to guard
  the church and the new parsonage being built next to the Revis house. **James Revis's family closed in
  their own front porch in 1957 so the guards could watch from it.** His daughter **Laverne Revis
  Martin**: "[m]y parents had a little room that they closed out on the front of the porch just so the
  guards would be able to watch." Her older sister **Robbie Revis Smith**: "they decided that they needed
  to close in the front porch so they could see better . . . with our house sitting on the corner, it
  gave a very good view several ways that they could see because they had the glass out there and they
  put a little gas heater out there so they wouldn't be too cold sitting up. They did this year round."
  The volunteers were called the **Civil Rights Guards**, and they worked **shifts of 6 p.m. to midnight
  and midnight to 6 a.m.** Robbie Revis Smith on what that was like to come home to: "So it was kind of
  a good feeling coming home because you never came home without a person out there watching you, so you
  didn't have to worry about that." **Laverne Revis Martin also recorded that women in the movement took
  a weekly shift in the guardhouse**, among them **Desta Brooks**, a florist on the movement's executive
  committee alongside James Revis, and **Minnie Eaton**, a schoolteacher. Guards were also posted in a
  shack behind the parsonage to watch the back of the property.
  **It was Laverne who spotted the second bomb, coming home from work, and alerted the guards.**
  (Interviews: Laverne Revis Martin by Dr. Horace Huntley of the Birmingham Civil Rights Institute, **17
  July 1996**, and by Marjorie White, **12 April 2004**, and by Susan C. Salvatore by telephone, **9
  February 2004**; Robbie Revis Smith by Dr. Horace Huntley at Miles College, **1 November 1995**. All
  cited in the NHL nomination, footnotes 37-41.)

### 9k. STORY — the Revis family of Bethel Baptist Church (`revis-family-bethel`) — the `civil-rights-congregation` TARGET SLOT IS FILLED, AND REPLACED

**The slot asked for "a congregation that housed a civil rights campaign."** `hb-story` blocks are for
named people, so the slot is filled by the named family whose house became the church's guardhouse.
Every fact is in §9j above, from the National Park Service's National Historic Landmark nomination and
the oral-history interviews it cites. **No composite. Three real people, all named in a federal
document.**

- **James Revis**, a deacon of Bethel Baptist Church, Birmingham, and a member of the ACMHR executive
  committee, whose house stood across the street from the church.
- **Laverne Revis Martin** and **Robbie Revis Smith**, his daughters, both recorded on tape by the
  Birmingham Civil Rights Institute in the 1990s.
- The family closed in their own front porch in 1957 to make a guard post, with glass and a small gas
  heater, and the church's men and women watched the church from it in six-hour shifts all year round.
- Laverne saw the second bomb on her way home from work at about half past one in the morning on Sunday
  29 June 1958 and alerted the guards, who carried it into the street before it went off.
- **Movie:** none verified. `movie=""`.
- **Shared with:** `rights-movements` (the Birmingham campaign, the Freedom Rides and the 16th Street
  Baptist Church bombing — none of it repeated here).

### 9l. More religions, and the law that finally said Native religion was allowed

**Post-1965 arrivals.** `immigration` and `government-politics` LEAD on the Immigration and Nationality
Act of 1965 as law and as arrival. This chapter's angle is the religions that arrived with the people.
**The size of the change is only measurable from a pollster, and the numbers are era-10 numbers, so they
are carried in §10 below and labelled there.** Nothing about the size of these communities in the 1970s
or 1980s was sourced from this environment, and **the outline does not assert a figure for those
decades.**

**THE AMERICAN INDIAN RELIGIOUS FREEDOM ACT — the direct continuation of §7d and §8g of this bank, which
carry the 1883 Rules Governing the Court of Indian Offenses and the punishment of ceremonies inside
living memory.**

- **Public Law 95-341, a joint resolution, approved 11 August 1978** (92 Stat. 469,
  https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-92/pdf/STATUTE-92-Pg469.pdf ). Congress's own findings,
  quoted exactly, are the strongest sentences available for this cell: "the lack of a clear,
  comprehensive, and consistent Federal policy has often resulted in the abridgment of religious freedom
  for traditional American Indians"; "such laws and policies often deny American Indians access to sacred
  sites required in their religions, including cemeteries"; "such laws at times prohibit the use and
  possession of sacred objects necessary to the exercise of religious rites and ceremonies"; and
  **"traditional American Indian ceremonies have been intruded upon, interfered with, and in a few
  instances banned."**
- **What it enacted:** "henceforth it shall be the policy of the United States to protect and preserve
  for American Indians their inherent right of freedom to believe, express, and exercise the traditional
  religions of the American Indian, Eskimo, Aleut, and Native Hawaiians, including but not limited to
  access to sites, use and possession of sacred objects, and the freedom to worship through ceremonials
  and traditional rites." Section 2 told the President to have federal agencies review their rules in
  consultation with native traditional religious leaders and report back to Congress in twelve months.
- **It was a statement of policy and it did not settle the question. *Employment Division, Department of
  Human Resources of Oregon v. Smith*, 494 U.S. 872, argued 6 November 1989 and decided 17 April 1990.**
  From the Court's own syllabus: "Respondents Smith and Black were fired by a private drug rehabilitation
  organization because they ingested peyote, a hallucinogenic drug, for sacramental purposes at a
  ceremony of their Native American Church." Oregon refused them unemployment pay for work-related
  misconduct. **The Court held that the Free Exercise Clause permits a state to ban sacramental peyote
  use and to deny the benefits.** (https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep494/usrep494872/usrep494872.pdf )
- **Congress answered in 1994. The American Indian Religious Freedom Act Amendments of 1994, Public Law
  103-344, approved 6 October 1994** (108 Stat. 3125,
  https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-108/pdf/STATUTE-108-Pg3125.pdf ). Congress's findings:
  "for many Indian people, the traditional ceremonial use of the peyote cactus as a religious sacrament
  has for centuries been integral to a way of life"; "since 1965, this ceremonial use of peyote by
  Indians has been protected by Federal regulation"; **28 states had matching laws and 22 did not**; and
  the Supreme Court in *Smith* "held that the First Amendment does not protect Indian practitioners who
  use peyote in Indian religious ceremonies." **What it enacted:** "the use, possession, or
  transportation of peyote by an Indian for bona fide traditional ceremonial purposes in connection with
  the practice of a traditional Indian religion is lawful, and shall not be prohibited by the United
  States or any State."
- **`native-nations` LEADS on what these bans did to nations.** This chapter's slice is the religion: that
  it took an Act of Congress in 1978 to say that a Native person could worship, and another in 1994 to
  make one ceremony lawful in every state.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): the peyote case by name, the 1988 sacred-land case, RFRA 1993 and its limits, eagle feathers 1962

**Why:** §9l names the *Smith* respondents only as "Smith and Black" and has nothing between 1978 and 1994
except *Smith*. A writer will need the men's full names, the vote, the sacred-land case that came first,
and what Congress did in 1993. All four read in full from the primary documents.

- **The two men in *Smith*.** "Respondents Alfred Smith and Galen Black (hereinafter respondents) were
  fired from their jobs with a private drug rehabilitation organization because they ingested peyote for
  sacramental purposes at a ceremony of the Native American Church, of which both are members." Oregon
  law made possessing peyote a Class B felony with no exception for sacramental use. (494 U.S. 872, at
  874, https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep494/usrep494872/usrep494872.pdf )
- **The vote, from the printed lineup.** Justice Scalia wrote for the Court, joined by Rehnquist, White,
  Stevens and Kennedy. Justice O'Connor concurred in the judgment only. **Justice Blackmun dissented,
  joined by Brennan and Marshall.** So the judgment was 6 to 3, and the reasoning was 5 to 4. (Same
  source, syllabus.)
- **The majority's own words on who loses.** Scalia: "leaving accommodation to the political process
  will place at a relative disadvantage those religious practices that are not widely engaged in; but
  that unavoidable consequence of democratic government must be preferred to a system in which each
  conscience is a law unto itself." (494 U.S. 872, at 890.)
- **Blackmun's dissent, on the 1978 Act:** "The American Indian Religious Freedom Act, in itself, may not
  create rights enforceable against government action restricting religious freedom, but this Court must
  scrupulously apply its free exercise analysis to the religious claims of Native Americans, however
  unorthodox they may be. Otherwise, both the First Amendment and the stated policy of Congress will offer
  to Native Americans merely an unfulfilled and hollow promise." (494 U.S. 872, Blackmun, J., dissenting.)
- **The sacred-land case two years earlier: *Lyng v. Northwest Indian Cemetery Protective Association*,
  485 U.S. 439, argued 30 November 1987, decided 19 April 1988.** Who and what: the **U.S. Forest
  Service** (Secretary of Agriculture Richard Lyng was the named petitioner) planned a **6-mile paved
  segment** to finish a 75-mile road between **Gasquet and Orleans, California** (the "G-O road"),
  through the **Chimney Rock** area of **Six Rivers National Forest**, and a plan to log the area. The
  area "has historically been used for religious purposes by **Yurok, Karok, and Tolowa** Indians." The
  Forest Service's own commissioned study (the Theodoratus Report, 1979, 423 pages) found that a road on
  any available route "would cause serious and irreparable damage to the sacred areas" and recommended
  that it not be built. **In 1982 the Forest Service rejected that recommendation** and chose a route
  through Chimney Rock. The Court, **5 to 3** (O'Connor for the Court with Rehnquist, White, Stevens and
  Scalia; **Brennan dissenting with Marshall and Blackmun; Kennedy took no part**), held that the Free
  Exercise Clause did not stop the road or the logging: "Even assuming that the Government's actions here
  will virtually destroy the Indians' ability to practice their religion, the Constitution simply does not
  provide a principle that could justify upholding respondents' legal claims." It also held that the
  1978 Act has not "so much as a hint of any intent to create a cause of action or any judicially
  enforceable individual rights." Brennan's dissent: the Yurok, Karok and Tolowa had held the area
  sacred "for at least 200 years and probably much longer," about **25 square miles**, known to them as
  the "high country"; its most powerful sites are Chimney Rock, Doctor Rock and Peak 8. **The Court's own
  opinion records that "the Indians themselves were far from unanimous in opposing the G-O road."**
  (485 U.S. 439, syllabus, opinion of the Court and Brennan, J., dissenting,
  https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep485/usrep485439/usrep485439.pdf )
  Whether the road was ever built: Congress added the corridor to wilderness in 1990 and the segment was
  never completed (unconfirmed: search summary only). **Do not write the ending unless a writer opens a
  source for it.**
- **Land (brief item 5):** the Chimney Rock high country is Yurok, Karuk (the opinion spells it Karok)
  and Tolowa land, held by the Forest Service as federal land. Same source.
- **Congress answers *Smith*, 1993: the Religious Freedom Restoration Act, Public Law 103-141, approved
  16 November 1993** (107 Stat. 1488, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-107/pdf/STATUTE-107-Pg1488.pdf ).
  Finding (4): "in Employment Division v. Smith, 494 U.S. 872 (1990) the Supreme Court virtually
  eliminated the requirement that the government justify burdens on religious exercise imposed by laws
  neutral toward religion." What it enacted: "Government shall not substantially burden a person's
  exercise of religion even if the burden results from a rule of general applicability," unless the
  burden furthers "a compelling governmental interest" by "the least restrictive means." Its stated
  purpose was to restore the test of *Sherbert v. Verner* (1963) and *Wisconsin v. Yoder* (1972).
- **The Court cut it back in 1997: *City of Boerne v. Flores*, 521 U.S. 507, decided 25 June 1997.** The
  Catholic **Archbishop of San Antonio (Patrick Flores)** was refused a permit to enlarge a church in
  **Boerne, Texas** under a historic-preservation ordinance and sued under RFRA. **Held: "RFRA exceeds
  Congress' power"** as applied to the states. Kennedy wrote for the Court; O'Connor, Souter and Breyer
  dissented. (521 U.S. 507, syllabus,
  https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521507/usrep521507.pdf ) The
  1994 peyote amendment in §9l is a separate statute and was not touched by *Boerne*.
- **Eagle feathers after 1940.** Public Law 87-884, **approved 24 October 1962** (76 Stat. 1246,
  https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-76/pdf/STATUTE-76-Pg1246.pdf ), extended the 1940 Act to
  the golden eagle and set the penalty for taking or possessing any part of either eagle without a permit
  at **up to $500 or six months in prison**. The same law let the Secretary of the Interior permit the
  taking and possession of eagles "for the religious purposes of Indian tribes." So from 1962 a Native
  person needed a federal permit to hold an eagle feather for worship. That prosecutions of traditional
  practitioners happened is in §8 (Irwin, at 7). **No named prosecution of a practitioner for a religious
  feather was sourced here; do not name one.**

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): who killed members of the churches, 1958 to 1999 (the era-zoom claim)

**Why:** the outline's era zoom says men "bombed their buildings and killed their members." The deaths
are in `rights-movements`, not in this bank. Pointer and counts, so the religion writer can source the
sentence without retelling the 1963 story.

- **16th Street Baptist Church, Birmingham, 15 September 1963. `rights-movements` LEADS and carries it
  in full** (`research/research-rights-movements.md`, the NPS "16th Street Baptist Church Bombing (1963)"
  and "Four Little Girls" pages): a bomb under the church steps "just before 11 o'clock" killed **Addie
  Mae Collins, 14, Denise McNair, 11, Carole Robertson, 14, and Cynthia Wesley, 14.** Convictions:
  **Robert Chambliss, 14 November 1977; Thomas Blanton, 1 May 2001; Bobby Frank Cherry, 2002.** Herman
  Cash died in 1994 and was never prosecuted. **Injured: `rights-movements` has "seventeen others" from a
  search summary only. Jason Wollschleger (sociologist, Whitworth University), The Conversation, page
  dated 19 August 2026, says the bomb "killed four girls and injured 22 others."** Two counts; give both
  or neither.
- **How many attacks on Black churches there were in the movement years.** Wollschleger's research team
  has mapped "about 145 cases across the South, ranging from around 1954 to 1970," and he records that in
  Albany, Georgia, in 1961-62 "four nearby Black churches associated with the Student Nonviolent
  Coordinating Committee were burned to the ground, including Shady Grove Baptist and High Hope Baptist
  churches." (https://theconversation.com/birmingham-church-bombing-wasnt-an-isolated-act-of-terrorism-there-were-dozens-of-attacks-on-black-houses-of-worship-during-the-civil-rights-movement-288674 )
  **This is one research team's count, labelled as such; it is not an official figure.**
- **A synagogue too: The Temple, Atlanta, 12 October 1958.** "In the early hours of October 12, 1958,
  fifty sticks of dynamite exploded in a recessed entranceway at the Hebrew Benevolent Congregation."
  "No people were hurt or killed." Within days five men, all members of antisemitic hate groups, were
  arrested; only **George Bright** was tried. His first trial ended in a mistrial with the jury split 9 to
  3; his second, with the lawyer Reuben Garland, ended in acquittal, and the charges against the other
  four were dropped. **No one was ever convicted.** The congregation's rabbi, **Jacob Rothschild**, had
  preached against segregation from his pulpit since Yom Kippur 1947. (Edward A. Hatfield, University of
  Georgia, New Georgia Encyclopedia, "Temple Bombing," published 1 June 2007, last edited 15 July 2020,
  https://www.georgiaencyclopedia.org/articles/arts-culture/temple-bombing/ ) The Temple is the same
  congregation Leo Frank belonged to (era 8 PATCH).
- **Nine killed at a Buddhist temple, 1991.** On **10 August 1991** nine people were shot dead at **Wat
  Promkunaram**, a Thai Buddhist temple near Phoenix, Arizona (at Waddell): **six monks, a nun, a novice
  and a temple boy**, found in a circle, each shot in the head. The killing was done in a robbery of the
  monks. **Johnathan Doody**, 17 at the time, was convicted on nine counts of first-degree murder at his
  third trial and sentenced on **14 March 2014 to 249 years**; a first conviction had been thrown out
  because a federal appeals court found his confession coerced. His co-defendant **Alessandro (Alex)
  Garcia** pleaded guilty and was sentenced to life. (NBC News, 14 March 2014, journalism,
  https://www.nbcnews.com/news/us-news/1991-temple-killer-sentenced-249-years-prison-n52981 )
- **The 1990s church fires, from the federal task force's own figures.** Congress passed the **Church
  Arson Prevention Act of 1996, Public Law 104-155, approved 3 July 1996** (110 Stat. 1392,
  https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-110/pdf/STATUTE-110-Pg1392.pdf ). Finding (2): "The
  incidence of arson of places of religious worship has recently increased, especially in the context of
  places of religious worship that serve predominantly African-American congregations." The **National
  Church Arson Task Force** (Justice and Treasury) reported **297 arsons, bombings or attempted bombings
  at houses of worship in 1996, 209 in 1997, 165 in 1998 and 140 in 1999**; **945 investigations** of
  incidents between 1 January 1995 and 15 August 2000; **431 suspects arrested**; **305 defendants
  convicted** in connection with 224 arsons or bombings. **Jay Scott Ballinger pleaded guilty on 11 July
  2000 to setting 26 churches on fire in eight states between 1994 and 1999.** (U.S. Department of
  Justice press release 00-542, 15 September 2000,
  https://www.justice.gov/archive/opa/pr/2000/September/542cr.htm ) The task force's final report put
  **33 per cent** of the attacks from January 1995 to August 2000 at African American churches
  (unconfirmed: search summary only).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): Waco, 1993, who fired and the counts

**Why:** a religious community besieged by federal agents, with deaths on both sides and children killed.
Nothing in any chapter's bank. Two official sources, which do not agree on every number. **Every count
below carries its source.**

- **Who they were.** The **Branch Davidians**, a religious community at the **Mount Carmel** property
  outside **Waco, Texas**, led by **Vernon Howell, who called himself David Koresh**. (House Report
  104-749, 1996, https://www.congress.gov/104/crpt/hrpt749/CRPT-104hrpt749.pdf )
- **28 February 1993.** "a force of **76 ATF agents** stormed the Davidian residence to serve the arrest
  and search warrants." The Davidians had learned of the raid in advance. The gun battle "continued for
  almost 90 minutes. **Four ATF agents were killed** in the battle and **more than 20 agents wounded**. At
  least two Davidians were killed by ATF agents and several others, including Koresh, were wounded."
  (House Report 104-749, majority summary.) The four agents: **Conway C. LeBleu, Todd W. McKeehan,
  Robert J. Williams and Steven D. Willis**; 20 agents wounded (same report, minority views). The
  Special Counsel counts **six Davidians killed on 28 February** (Danforth, below, note 1).
- **WHO FIRED FIRST. Say that it is disputed, and say what each side says.** ATF agents testified that
  the Davidians fired first; Texas Ranger Captain **David Byrnes** told the subcommittees "there is no
  doubt who fired first." The Davidians' lawyers testified that they believed the battle began with an
  accidental discharge by an ATF weapon. The foreman of the criminal-trial jury told a member that the
  jury "could not decide because the evidence was in such conflict as to who fired first." **The
  subcommittees' finding: "The evidence suggests that the Davidians fired the first shots... the question
  of who fired the first shot on February 28 cannot decisively be resolved... It appears more likely,
  however, that the Davidians fired first."** (House Report 104-749, finding 5, and the testimony at its notes 117-119.)
- **The 51 days.** The FBI took over and negotiated for 51 days. Between late February and 23 March,
  **35 people, including 21 children**, came out (majority report); the minority views give **14 children
  and 21 adults**. Two counts; give both or neither. From 23 March no one else left. A girl of six,
  **Melissa Morrison**, told an FBI negotiator she could not leave because "David won't let me"; she died
  in the fire. (House Report 104-749, minority views.)
- **19 April 1993.** At about 6 a.m. FBI armoured vehicles began punching holes in the walls and spraying
  tear gas in; the Davidians fired on the vehicles. At about 12.07 p.m. a fire was seen, two more began
  within two minutes, and within eight minutes the building was engulfed. (House Report 104-749.) **"More
  than 70 Davidians, including 22 children, died"** that day (same report).
- **WHO STARTED THE FIRE AND WHO SHOT THE PEOPLE INSIDE, from the Special Counsel.** Former Senator **John
  C. Danforth**, appointed Special Counsel by the Attorney General, examined the case for ten months.
  **Interim Report, 21 July 2000** (https://www.cesnur.org/testi/DanforthRpt.pdf ): "Government agents
  did not start or spread the tragic fire of April 19, 1993, did not direct gunfire at the Branch
  Davidians." Responsibility "rests with certain of the Branch Davidians and their leader, David Koresh,
  who: (a) shot and killed four ATF agents on February 28, 1993, and wounded 20 others; ... (c) directed
  gunfire at FBI agents who were inserting tear gas into the complex on April 19, 1993; (d) spread fuel
  throughout the main structure of the complex and ignited it in at least three places ...; and (e)
  killed some of their own people by gunfire, including at least five children." **20 Davidians died of
  gunshot wounds on 19 April**, many shot in the head or mouth at close range, and a three-year-old,
  **Dayland Gent**, was stabbed in the chest. **Total dead: "at least 80"**, counting the six killed on 28
  February and not counting two unborn children; the exact number cannot be known because of the fire.
  **The report also states that an FBI agent fired three pyrotechnic tear-gas rounds at 8.08 a.m. on 19
  April** at a construction pit about 75 feet from the living quarters, and that the government did not
  disclose this until August 1999; it found those rounds did not start the fire four hours later. The
  Final Report of **8 November 2000** reaffirmed the interim findings (search summary of the final
  report; the interim report was read in full).
- **The trials.** Eleven Davidians were tried; a Texas jury convicted **eight** of various firearm
  offences, and seven of the eight were sentenced to 40 years. (House Report 104-749, minority views.)
- **Boundary:** this chapter's angle is a religious community and its end. `crime-justice` or
  `government-politics` may want the raid and the hearings as law enforcement. Listed in the checkpoint's
  TO PARK.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): Jonestown, 1978, an American congregation that died abroad

**Why:** a hard subject with a named leader and counts, and the congregation was American (Indianapolis,
then San Francisco). Short, labelled, and bounded. No superlative about it is sourced here.

- **Peoples Temple** was founded in **Indianapolis in the 1950s** by **Jim Jones** and moved to **San
  Francisco in 1971**; members then moved to a settlement at **Jonestown, Guyana**. On **18 November
  1978** Temple members shot dead **Congressman Leo Ryan** of California and four others at the
  **Port Kaituma airstrip**, among them an NBC cameraman and a Hearst newspapers photographer. At Jones's
  order, **909 members died at Jonestown, all but two of apparent cyanide poisoning**; children were given
  it first by syringe, by parents and nurses. (Association for Diplomatic Studies and Training, "The
  Jonestown Massacre," November 2012, https://adst.org/2012/11/the-jonestown-massacre/ , an oral-history
  organisation of former U.S. diplomats.)
- **Counts that disagree:** ADST says "over 200" children; a search summary gives "some 300" aged 17 or
  under, and 918 dead in all across Jonestown, the airstrip and the Temple's house in Georgetown
  (unconfirmed: search summary only). **Name the numbers with their sources, or leave the child count
  out.**
- **Not in the outline.** The outline does not mention Jonestown. A writer who adds it must keep it to
  what the sources say.

- Every dated claim in the outline's 1950-2000 cell was checked against §9a-9l and the T-260c PATCHes above.
