### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-27 (T-260c): who set the Bethel Baptist bombs of 25 December 1956 and 14 December 1962?
Sources checked: the NHL nomination (it names J. B. Stoner for 1958 only and says the 1958 bombing "was
not solved until 1980"); NPS "The Historic Bethel Baptist Church"; the King Institute's Shuttlesworth
entry; The Conversation (Wollschleger 2026), which names no perpetrator for Bethel; search results for
the 1956 and 1962 bombings (none reports a charge or conviction). The 1962 date, 14 December, appears
only in a search summary.
How the prose can say it: "No one is recorded as convicted of the 1956 bomb or the 1962 bomb."

### PATCH 2026-09-30 (T-327b): cyanide, defined (clinical-word rule, for Jonestown 1978)
- Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry (CDC), *Medical Management Guidelines for Hydrogen
  Cyanide* (https://wwwn.cdc.gov/tsp/MMG/MMGDetails.aspx?mmgid=1141&toxid=249 , fetched 2026-09-30): "By
  binding to mitochondrial cytochrome oxidase, it prevents the utilization of oxygen in cellular
  metabolism." "In humans, cyanide combines with the ferric ion in mitochondrial cytochrome oxidase ...
  bringing oxidative phosphorylation and ATP production to a halt."
- Plain words for the prose: cyanide is a poison that stops the cells of the body from using oxygen, so
  the cells cannot make the energy they need to live.

### PATCH 2026-10-02 (T-480): era 9, what became of the 1962 school-prayer amendments
- National Archives, "The Constitution: Amendments 11-27," https://www.archives.gov/founding-docs/amendments-11-27
  (WebFetch 2026-10-02): the amendments ratified after 1962 are the 24th (poll taxes, 1964), 25th
  (presidential succession, 1967), 26th (voting age 18, 1971) and 27th (congressional pay, 1992). **None
  concerns prayer or religion in schools.** So none of the amendments proposed in June 1962 (Taylor, Beall,
  Johnson; §9b) became part of the Constitution. (Whether any passed either house is not sourced here; the
  prose says only that none became part of the Constitution.)

## ERA 10 — 2000 to Today (`state="full"`)

**THE PERISHABLE-FACTS RULE GOVERNS THIS WHOLE SECTION.** Every figure below carries the year it
describes **and** the year of its source. Nothing here says "today," "currently," or "in recent years."
Where the newest confirmable number is a few years old, that is said plainly. Pollsters are labelled as
pollsters. **All web sources in this section were read on 10 September 2026.**

### 10a. [VERIFY] CLEARED — the era frame: how many people say they have no religion

**Pew Research Center is a polling organisation, not a government count. Say so in the book.** Its
Religious Landscape Study surveyed **36,908 U.S. adults between 17 July 2023 and 4 March 2024** and was
**published on 26 February 2025.**

- **Share of U.S. adults who identify as Christian: 78 per cent in 2007, 71 per cent in 2014, 62 per
  cent in the 2023-24 study.**
- **Share who are religiously unaffiliated — atheist, agnostic, or "nothing in particular," the group
  usually called the "nones": 16 per cent in 2007, 23 per cent in 2014, 29 per cent in 2023-24.**
- **In the 2023-24 study: Jewish 1.7 per cent, Muslim 1.2 per cent, Buddhist 1.1 per cent, Hindu 0.9 per
  cent, other non-Christian religions 0.3 per cent.**
- **Pew's own headline finding is that the fall has slowed.** The report is titled "Decline of
  Christianity in the U.S. Has Slowed, May Have Leveled Off," and says the Christian share has been
  roughly stable for the past four or five years. **Do not write that Christianity is collapsing. Write
  what the pollster found, with the years.**
  (https://www.pewresearch.org/religion/2025/02/26/decline-of-christianity-in-the-us-has-slowed-may-have-leveled-off/ )
- **The membership figures continue §9h.** Gallup: **70 per cent of adults said they belonged to a
  church, synagogue or mosque in 1999; 50 per cent in 2018; 47 per cent in 2020** — the first time it
  had fallen below half since Gallup began asking in 1937. Three-year averages: **69 per cent in
  1998-2000, 62 per cent in 2008-2010, 49 per cent in 2018-2020**, with the share giving no religious
  preference going **8, 13, 21 per cent** over the same periods. By generation in 2018-2020:
  **Traditionalists born before 1946, 66 per cent; Baby Boomers, 58; Generation X, 50; Millennials,
  36.** (Jeffrey M. Jones, Gallup, 29 March 2021,
  https://news.gallup.com/poll/341963/church-membership-falls-below-majority-first-time.aspx )

### 10b. [VERIFY] CLEARED — public money reaching religious schools: the cases, the years, the states

**`government-politics` LEADS AND ALREADY HAS THE LAW.** `outlines/government-politics.md:419` carries
all four rulings as law: *Zelman v. Simmons-Harris*, 27 June 2002 (Cleveland's vouchers, worth up to
$2,250 a year, with 82 per cent of participating private schools religious); *Trinity Lutheran Church of
Columbia v. Comer*, 26 June 2017 (a Missouri church preschool and a playground-surfacing grant);
*Espinoza v. Montana Department of Revenue*, 30 June 2020, five to four; and *Carson v. Makin*, 21 June
2022 (Maine). `education` at :1017-1021 carries vouchers, education savings accounts and tax-credit
scholarships as school choice, with EdChoice's count of 69 programmes and about 1,162,000 children for
the 2024-25 school year. **Do not retell any of that.**

**What is left, and what belongs to this chapter, is what the schools themselves teach — which is in
*Carson v. Makin*'s own record.** Maine pays tuition for children in towns with no secondary school of
their own, and since 1981 had paid it only to "nonsectarian" schools.

- **The families are named in the opinion.** **David and Amy Carson of Glenburn, Maine**, whose daughter
  attended **Bangor Christian Schools**, founded in 1970 as a ministry of Bangor Baptist Church; they
  paid her fees themselves. **Troy and Angela Nelson of Palermo, Maine**, whose son attended **Temple
  Academy**, affiliated with Centerpoint Community Church, and whose daughter attended a secular private
  school because they could not afford Temple Academy's fees for both children. (596 U.S. 767, at 4-5,
  https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/21pdf/20-1088_dbfi.pdf )
- **What the two schools say they are for, quoted in Justice Breyer's dissent from the record.** Bangor
  Christian's stated educational objectives include "lead[ing] each unsaved student to trust Christ as
  his/her personal savior and then to follow Christ as Lord of his/her life"; the school "does not
  believe there is any way to separate the religious instruction from the academic instruction," and
  teaches in social studies "that God has ordained evangelism" and in science that atmospheric layers
  "are evidence of God's good design." Temple Academy's objectives include "foster[ing] within each
  student an attitude of love and reverence of the Bible as the infallible, inerrant, and authoritative
  Word of God," and it "provides a 'biblically-integrated education,' which means that the Bible is used
  in every subject that is taught" — in mathematics, that "a creator designed the universe such that
  'one plus one is always going to be two.'" (596 U.S. 767, Breyer, J., dissenting, at 11-12.)
- **The vote was 6 to 3.** Chief Justice Roberts wrote the opinion, joined by Thomas, Alito, Gorsuch,
  Kavanaugh and Barrett. Breyer dissented, joined by Kagan and (in part) Sotomayor, who also dissented
  separately.
- **This is the plain point for a young reader, and it is not an argument, it is what the record says:**
  the question in these cases was whether a state that pays for children's schooling may refuse to pay
  when the school teaches a religion in every lesson. **After 2022 the answer in Maine was no.**

### 10c. [VERIFY] CLEARED — the 2022 ruling on a coach praying at a school game

***Kennedy v. Bremerton School District*, 597 U.S. 507, argued 25 April 2022 and decided 27 June 2022.**
Read in full from https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/21pdf/21-418_i425.pdf

- **Joseph Kennedy began coaching football at Bremerton High School in Washington State in 2008**, after
  nearly two decades in the Marine Corps. He gave thanks by prayer on the field at the end of each game,
  after the players and coaches had shaken hands, by taking a knee at the 50-yard line and praying for
  about thirty seconds. (597 U.S. 507, slip op. at 2.)
- **At first he prayed alone.** Over time players asked whether they could pray with him and he said,
  "This is a free country. You can do what you want." The number who joined him grew to most of the team
  after some games; sometimes players from the other team were invited; sometimes he still prayed alone.
  He later began adding short motivational speeches to the prayer when others were there. Separately, the
  team sometimes prayed in the locker room, which the opinion calls a school tradition older than his
  time there. (Slip op. at 2-3.)
- **Nobody complained for more than seven years.** The district's superintendent learned of it only in
  **September 2015**, after an employee of another school praised the practice to Bremerton's principal.
  The district wrote to him on **17 September 2015.** (Slip op. at 3.)
- **The three games.** After a letter of **16 October** forbidding "any overt actions" that could appear
  to endorse prayer while on duty, he prayed after the games of **16, 23 and 26 October 2015**. On 16
  October most Bremerton players were singing the school fight song; players from the other team and
  members of the public joined him before he finished. On 23 October "no one joined him." On 26 October
  other adults gathered around him while he knelt. **After the 26 October game the district put him on
  paid administrative leave** and barred him from taking any part in the football programme, in a letter
  criticising "public and demonstrative religious conduct while still on duty as an assistant coach."
  (Slip op. at 4-7.)
- **The Court ruled for him, six to three.** Justice Gorsuch wrote the opinion, joined by Roberts,
  Thomas, Alito and Barrett, and by Kavanaugh except as to one part. Held: "The Free Exercise and Free
  Speech Clauses of the First Amendment protect an individual engaging in a personal religious
  observance from government reprisal."
- **THE DISPUTE IS PART OF THE FACT, AND THE BOOK MUST SAY SO.** Justice Sotomayor, dissenting with
  Breyer and Kagan: "To the degree the Court portrays petitioner Joseph Kennedy's prayers as private and
  quiet, it misconstrues the facts. The record reveals that Kennedy had a longstanding practice of
  conducting demonstrative prayers on the 50-yard line of the football field. Kennedy consistently
  invited others to join his prayers and for years led student athletes in prayer at the same time and
  location." She also wrote that the case "is about whether a public school must permit a school
  official to kneel, bow his head, and say a prayer at the center of a school event."
  **Write it as what it is: the nine justices read the same record and described what happened
  differently, six to three.** Do not tell the reader which description is right, and do not say anything
  about what Joseph Kennedy, who is living, wanted or felt beyond what he is recorded as saying.

### 10d. STORY — Joseph Kennedy (`joseph-kennedy-bremerton`) — the `school-faith-dispute-account` TARGET SLOT IS FILLED

**The slot asked for "a family, teacher, or coach in a school-and-faith dispute" with an on-the-record
account.** Every fact is from the Supreme Court's own opinion and dissent, cited in §10c. **He is a
living person; nothing is said about his motives that the record does not carry in his own quoted
words.** The dissent's different account of the same events is part of the story, not a footnote to it.

### 10e. [VERIFY] CLEARED — state laws putting religious texts in classrooms

**LOUISIANA IS THE CLEAREST CASE AND ITS LAW IS STILL IN COURT. Source: the en banc opinion of the
United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit in *Roake v. Brumley*, No. 24-30706, filed 20
February 2026, https://www.ca5.uscourts.gov/opinions/pub/24/24-30706-CV1.pdf**

- **What the law requires.** Louisiana House Bill 71, now La. R.S. § 17:2124(B), says that "No later
  than January 1, 2025, each public school governing authority shall display the Ten Commandments in
  each classroom in each school under its jurisdiction," on **a poster or framed document at least
  eleven inches by fourteen inches**, with the text "the central focus" and "printed in a large, easily
  readable font." The statute then **prints the exact wording that must be used**, beginning "I AM the
  LORD thy God." Schools must also post a "context statement" on the "History of the Ten Commandments in
  American Public Education," and **may** display the Mayflower Compact, the Declaration of Independence
  and the Northwest Ordinance alongside it, but **only the Ten Commandments are required.**
- **This chapter's angle, and it is exactly the *Schempp* argument of 1963 again.** The people suing
  include ministers — the case name comes from the **Reverend Darcy Roake** — and Jewish parents. From
  Judge Higginson's dissenting opinion, quoting a Jewish parent's sworn statement in the district court:
  the law "(1) misappropriates a Jewish text, ripping it from its Jewish context, (2) selectively edits
  that text by altering its meaning and obscuring or erasing its Jewish significance, and (3) then
  mandates the display of the altered text to non-Jews, in violation of core Jewish tenets that oppose
  proselytizing." Another Jewish parent objects that Louisiana's version leaves out the deliverance of
  the Jews from slavery in Egypt, which is "a key piece of the Ten Commandments and of the Jewish story."
  **A Presbyterian minister who is also a parent plaintiff objects to the state's "stamp of approval" for
  one particular version.** (Slip op., Higginson, J., dissenting, at 26-27 and notes 6-7.)
- **The 1980 precedent, which is era-9 material and should be told there or here as the writer prefers.**
  In ***Stone v. Graham*, 449 U.S. 39, decided 17 November 1980**, the Supreme Court struck down a
  Kentucky law requiring the Ten Commandments to be posted in every public classroom. Judge Dennis's
  dissent in *Roake* lists seven ways the Louisiana and Kentucky laws match, and says "A display meeting
  H.B. 71's requirements is materially indistinguishable from those struck down in *Stone*." Louisiana
  argued that *Stone* rested on the *Lemon* test, which it says *Kennedy v. Bremerton* abandoned in 2022.
  (Slip op., Dennis, J., dissenting, at 17-19.)
- **WHERE THE LAW STOOD WHEN THIS WAS RESEARCHED, AND SAY IT THIS PRECISELY.** A federal district court
  granted a preliminary injunction stopping the law; a three-judge panel of the Fifth Circuit affirmed
  on **20 June 2025**; the full Fifth Circuit reheard the case and **on 20 February 2026 vacated the
  injunction**. **The en banc court did not decide whether the law is constitutional.** Its own words:
  "The question before us, however, is not whether H.B. 71 is constitutional, but whether that issue is
  fit for judicial resolution at this time," and it held that "Because the parents' challenge turns on
  unresolved factual and contextual questions, equitable relief was premature." Judges Dennis,
  Higginson and Ramirez wrote dissents. **As of 10 September 2026 the question of whether the law
  violates the First Amendment had not been answered by any court. Write it as unsettled, because it is.**
- **Louisiana is not alone, and the *Roake* opinions name a Texas companion case** in which **Rabbi Mara
  Nathan** is a plaintiff, objecting that the required text "erases this foundational Jewish tenet and
  historical context." **No further state-by-state list was sourced from this environment, and the
  outline therefore names Louisiana and says Texas has a companion case, and asserts nothing about how
  many states have passed such laws.**

### 10f. [VERIFY] CLEARED — church schools now compared with their peak, told from the church's side

**`education` LEADS ON THE ENROLMENT NUMBERS and already has them** — `outlines/education.md:813` gives
the Catholic peak of **5,601,000 children in 13,249 schools in 1964-65** and the fall to 2,475,439 by
1990-91, and :1013-1015 gives **1.7 million Catholic-school children in the autumn of 2021** out of 4.7
million private-school children, published May 2024. **Do not retell those.**

**This chapter's angle is who was teaching in them, and that is a religion fact.** Source: the **Center
for Applied Research in the Apostolate (CARA) at Georgetown University**, "Frequently Requested Church
Statistics," spreadsheet `CARAFrequentlyRequested.xlsx` downloaded 10 September 2026 from
http://cara.georgetown.edu/faqs . CARA's figures come from *The Official Catholic Directory*, which
reports the state of the Church as of 1 January of the year it is published, so the column headed 2025
describes **1 January 2025.**

- **Religious sisters in the United States: 178,740 in 1965. 79,814 in 2000. 33,135 in 2025.**
- **Religious brothers: 12,096 in 1965; 3,290 in 2025.** **Total priests: 59,426 in 1965; 33,462 in
  2025.**
- **Catholic elementary schools: 9,366 in 1965; 4,606 in 2025. Catholic secondary schools: 1,986 in
  1965; 1,178 in 2025.**
- **Parishes: 17,763 in 1965; 16,125 in 2025. Parishes with no resident priest as pastor: 530 in 1965;
  3,674 in 2025.**
- **Catholics who say they attend Mass every week, from CARA's survey-based estimate: 54.9 per cent in
  1970; 17.6 per cent in 2025.** **This one is a survey estimate, not a count, and must be labelled.**
- **The plain sentence for the book: the second school system this chapter describes in 1850 to 1900 was
  built and staffed by members of religious orders, and there are now about one sister for every five
  there were in 1965.** (178,740 to 33,135 is a fall of about 81 per cent. State the two numbers; the
  percentage is arithmetic the writer may use or drop.)

### 10g. [VERIFY] CLEARED — religious homeschooling's share

**Source: U.S. Department of Education, National Center for Education Statistics, *Parent and Family
Involvement in Education: 2023*, First Look, NCES 2024-113, **published September 2024**, Table A-7,
from the Parent and Family Involvement in Education Survey of the 2023 National Household Education
Surveys Program. https://nces.ed.gov/pubs2024/2024113.pdf**

**Among children aged 5 to 17 who were homeschooled in the 2022-23 school year, the percentage whose
parents said each reason was important, and the percentage who said it was the most important:**

| Reason | Important | Most important |
|---|---|---|
| A concern about the environment of other schools | 83 | 28 |
| A desire to provide moral instruction | 75 | 6 |
| A desire to emphasize family life together | 72 | 8 |
| Dissatisfaction with academic instruction at other schools | 72 | 17 |
| **A desire to provide religious instruction** | **53** | **11** |
| A desire to provide a nontraditional approach | 50 | 8 |

**So: about half of homeschooling parents called religious instruction an important reason, and about
one in nine called it the most important one. Those are 2022-23 figures published in September 2024.**
`education` LEADS on how many children are homeschooled and on state oversight, and `home-family` LEADS
on homeschooling as household life.

### 10h. Clergy abuse of children in the Catholic Church, and its concealment

**THIS IS A HARD SUBJECT AND `control/hard-subjects-policy.md` BINDS IT. The clinical-word rule applies:
"sexual abuse" must be said in plain words at first use — adults using children's bodies for sex — and
the record's own categories must be given once, plainly, without lingering and without adjectives.**

**SOURCE 1 — the Church's own commissioned study.** John Jay College of Criminal Justice, *The Nature
and Scope of Sexual Abuse of Minors by Catholic Priests and Deacons in the United States, 1950-2002*,
commissioned by the United States Conference of Catholic Bishops and **published February 2004**, from
surveys of 195 dioceses (98 per cent of all diocesan priests) and 140 religious communities.
https://www.usccb.org/sites/default/files/issues-and-action/child-and-youth-protection/upload/The-Nature-and-Scope-of-Sexual-Abuse-of-Minors-by-Catholic-Priests-and-Deacons-in-the-United-States-1950-2002.pdf

- **4,392 priests had allegations of sexual abuse against them that were not withdrawn and not known to
  be false, for the years 1950 to 2002 — about 4 per cent of all priests active in those years.**
- **10,667 people made allegations.** Of those, 17.2 per cent had brothers or sisters who said they had
  been abused too.
- **The ages. Half of those who made allegations (50.9 per cent) were between 11 and 14 when it
  happened; 27.3 per cent were 15 to 17; 16 per cent were 8 to 10; and nearly 6 per cent were under 7.
  81 per cent were boys and 19 per cent were girls.**
- **What was done, in the report's own categories:** the most frequent acts alleged were touching over
  the child's clothing (52.6 per cent), touching under the child's clothes (44.9 per cent), the priest
  performing oral sex (26 per cent), the child undressed (25.7 per cent), and rape or attempted rape
  (the report's term is "penile penetration or attempted penile penetration," 22.4 per cent). Most
  abusers were alleged to have done more than one of these to the same child. **One plain sentence, and
  then move on. Do not describe an individual act.**
- **Where. In the priest's home or the parish house (40.9 per cent), in the church itself (16.3 per
  cent), in the child's own home (12.4 per cent), in a holiday house (10.3 per cent), in a school (10.3
  per cent), in a car (9.8 per cent).**
- **THE CONCEALMENT, in the same report's numbers. Only 384 of the 4,392 priests were ever criminally
  charged.** Fifteen per cent of the priests were reported to the police by a victim, and "a much
  smaller number were reported by a diocese or religious community." **Before 1993 only one third of the
  cases were known to Church officials at all**, and about a third of all the allegations in the study
  were made in 2002-2003. More than a quarter of allegations were made more than thirty years after the
  abuse began. Of priests with these allegations in their files, nearly 72 per cent were sent for
  evaluation or treatment and **no action at all was recorded for nearly 16 per cent.**
- **Money.** The dioceses and communities reported **$472 million** already paid for victims,
  compensation, treatment of priests and legal costs, and 14 per cent of them gave no figure; adding one
  publicly reported $85 million settlement takes it over **$500 million.** A table elsewhere in the same
  report totals **$572,507,094.**

**SOURCE 2 — a criminal investigation, in the grand jurors' own words.** Report 1 of the **Fortieth
Statewide Investigating Grand Jury of Pennsylvania**, released **August 2018**, covering the Catholic
dioceses of **Allentown, Erie, Greensburg, Harrisburg, Pittsburgh and Scranton** — 54 of Pennsylvania's
67 counties. https://www.pacourts.us/Storage/media/pdfs/20230515/193052-40thgrandjuryreport.pdf

- They read **half a million pages of internal diocesan documents** and heard dozens of witnesses. The
  documents "contained credible allegations against over three hundred predator priests. Over one
  thousand child victims were identifiable, from the church's own records. We believe that the real
  number — of children whose records were lost, or who were afraid ever to come forward — is in the
  thousands."
- Their own summary of what was done to the children: "Most of the victims were boys; but there were
  girls too. Some were teens; many were pre-pubescent. Some were manipulated with alcohol or
  pornography. Some were made to masturbate their assailants, or were groped by them. Some were raped
  orally, some vaginally, some anally. But all of them were brushed aside, in every part of the state,
  by church leaders who preferred to protect the abusers and their institution above all."
  **For an 8-15 reader, use the grand jury's first and last sentences of that passage and the fact that
  children were raped; do not reproduce the full list.**
- **"As a consequence of the coverup, almost every instance of abuse we found is too old to be
  prosecuted."**
- **THE CONCEALMENT SET OUT AS A LIST BY THE GRAND JURY, which it calls "a playbook for concealing the
  truth." Abbreviated but not softened:**
  1. "make sure to use euphemisms rather than real words to describe the sexual assaults in diocese
     documents. Never say 'rape'; say 'inappropriate contact' or 'boundary issues.'"
  2. do not investigate properly; have fellow clergy ask the questions about colleagues they live and
     work with.
  3. send the priest for "evaluation" at a church-run psychiatric centre and let it "diagnose" him
     largely from what he says about himself.
  4. "when a priest does have to be removed, don't say why. Tell his parishioners that he is on 'sick
     leave,' or suffering from 'nervous exhaustion.' Or say nothing at all."
  5. "even if a priest is raping children, keep providing him housing and living expenses."
  6. "if a predator's conduct becomes known to the community, don't remove him from the priesthood to
     ensure that no more children will be victimized. Instead, transfer him to a new location where no
     one will know he is a child abuser."
  7. "Finally and above all, don't tell the police."
  **Note for the writer: the grand jury's first item is euphemism. This book's own rule against
  euphemism is the same rule, and the passage teaches it without any comment from the narrator. Do not
  add the comment.**

**SOURCE 3 — how the counts stand now.** CARA's table (see §10f), "Clergy Sexual Abuse of Minors,"
counts allegations made **since 2004** by the decade in which the abuse is said to have happened:
**1960-69: 4,216. 1970-74: 2,813. 1975-79: 2,712. 1980-84: 2,113. 1985-89: 1,086. 1990-94: 516.
1995-99: 266. 2000-04: 179. 2005-09: 132. 2010-14: 136. 2015-19: 99. 2020-22: 36.** CARA's own note:
"The abuse allegation data are not equivalent to numbers of clergy accused. There are more allegations
than accused clergy as some of the priests have multiple victims making allegations of abuse against
them." **These are allegations reported later, not a count of what happened in each decade, and the book
must say so.**

**WHAT SET IT OFF, and what it is safe to say:** the John Jay study was commissioned by the bishops
after they adopted the **Charter for the Protection of Children and Young People in 2002**. **The
*Boston Globe*'s "Spotlight" reporting of January 2002 is what is usually credited with forcing the
issue into public view. No *Globe* article was retrieved from this environment, so the outline does not
quote one and does not describe the reporting; it says the study exists because the bishops
commissioned it in 2002.**

### 10i. Worshippers murdered where they were worshipping

**Every one of these is from a federal court record or a federal prosecutor's own case file. `war`,
`crime-justice` and `rights-movements` do not carry any of them. `rights-movements` LEADS on the 1963
bombing of the 16th Street Baptist Church, which is not retold here.**

- **Oak Creek, Wisconsin, Sunday 5 August 2012.** A white supremacist walked into the gurdwara — a Sikh
  house of worship — with a loaded gun and killed six people, among them **Paramjit Kaur**, who was
  sitting for morning prayers. The others were **Sita Singh, Ranjit Singh, Prakash Singh, Suvegh Singh
  Khattra and Satwant Singh Kaleka**, the gurdwara's president, who was killed fighting the gunman.
  **Santokh Singh and Punjab Singh were shot and survived.** (Testimony of **Harpreet Singh Saini**,
  Paramjit Kaur's son, before the Subcommittee on the Constitution, Civil Rights and Human Rights of the
  U.S. Senate Committee on the Judiciary, **19 September 2012**,
  https://www.judiciary.senate.gov/imo/media/doc/9-19-12SainiTestimony.pdf ) The full hearing, "Hate
  Crimes and the Threat of Domestic Extremism," is S. Hrg. 112-947,
  https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-112shrg20972/html/CHRG-112shrg20972.htm
- **Charleston, South Carolina, Wednesday 17 June 2015.** Twelve members of **Mother Emanuel** — Emanuel
  African Methodist Episcopal Church, the congregation this chapter's earlier eras follow from 1817 —
  met in the Fellowship Hall for their weekly Bible study. Dylann Storm Roof, then 21 years old (the opinion's own words, at 1), came in at about 8.16 in the
  evening. **"The parishioners welcomed Roof, handing him a Bible and a study sheet. For the next 45
  minutes, Roof worshipped with the parishioners. They stood and shut their eyes for closing prayer.
  Roof then took out his gun and started shooting."** He fired about seventy-four rounds, reloading
  several times. He told one woman who was praying aloud to "shut up," asked whether he had shot her
  yet, and said, "I'm going to leave you here to tell the story." **Nine people were killed: the Reverend
  Sharonda Coleman-Singleton, Cynthia Hurd, Susie Jackson, Ethel Lee Lance, the Reverend DePayne
  Middleton-Doctor, the Reverend Clementa Pinckney, Tywanza Sanders, the Reverend Daniel Simmons Sr. and
  the Reverend Myra Thompson.** He told the FBI he had chosen the church after looking up African
  American churches in Charleston, and had visited it beforehand and learned from a member that the
  Bible study met on Wednesday nights. (*United States v. Roof*, 10 F.4th 314 (4th Cir. 2021), decided
  25 August 2021, at 5-7, https://www.ca4.uscourts.gov/opinions/173.P.pdf )
- **Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, Saturday morning 27 October 2018.** **Three Jewish congregations — Tree of
  Life, Dor Hadash and New Light — shared one building** on Shabbat morning. Twelve Tree of Life members
  were in the Pervin Chapel in the middle of opening prayers. **Audrey Glickman**, 61, had opened the
  service with a prayer of gratitude and welcome. **David and Cecil Rosenthal**, 54 and 59, were
  brothers with significant developmental disabilities; David could not read but had memorised all the
  prayers and songs and regularly helped lead the service, and Cecil was one of the greeters. In Dor
  Hadash's space, **Jerry Rabinowitz**, a doctor, **Daniel Leger**, a hospital chaplain who had been a
  children's nurse for forty years, and **Martin Gaynor** were setting out prayer books and copies of
  the Torah. Downstairs in New Light's space, **Richard Gottfried** and **Daniel Stein** were in the
  kitchen making a meal for a men's breakfast the next day. Robert Bowers came in with three handguns
  and a rifle. **Eleven worshippers were killed: Jerry Rabinowitz, David Rosenthal, Cecil Rosenthal,
  Rose Mallinger (97), Bernice Simon (84), Sylvan Simon (86), Joyce Fienberg (75), Irving Younger (69),
  Richard Gottfried (65), Daniel Stein (71) and Melvin Wax (87).** Two more congregants were critically
  injured, twelve escaped unhurt, and five police officers were injured going in. **The federal charges
  included eleven counts of "obstruction of free exercise of religious beliefs resulting in death."** He
  was sentenced to death on 3 August 2023.
  (U.S. Attorney's Office, Western District of Pennsylvania, case page *US v. Robert Bowers*,
  https://www.justice.gov/usao-wdpa/vw/us-v-bowers ; names and ages from the United States' answering
  brief in the Third Circuit, No. 24-9002, https://justice.gov/crt/media/1436436/dl?inline= , at 5-13
  and 55. **A note on one name: that brief spells it "Joyce Feinberg" in one place and "Joyce Fienberg"
  in another. Use Fienberg and do not invent certainty.**)
- **The number the FBI counted after 11 September 2001.** In its own report *Hate Crime Statistics,
  2001*, the FBI records that anti-Islamic religious-bias incidents, "previously the second least
  reported," became the second highest reported religious bias in 2001, **"growing by more than 1,600
  percent over the 2000 volume."** In 2001 there were **481 incidents, 546 offences and 554 victims** of
  crimes motivated by bias against the Islamic religion. **Anti-Jewish incidents were still the highest**
  — 55.7 per cent of religious-bias offences and 56.5 per cent of religious-bias victims that year.
  (https://ucr.fbi.gov/hate-crime/2001/hatecrime01.pdf , pages 1 and following.)
- **A fact about counting, from Harpreet Singh Saini's testimony, and it is the sharpest line available
  for this cell.** In 2012 **the FBI did not track hate crimes against Sikhs at all.** He asked the
  Senate "to give my mother the dignity of being a statistic," and said, "My mother and those shot that
  day will not even count on a federal form. We cannot solve a problem we refuse to recognize."

### 10j. STORY — Harpreet Singh Saini (`harpreet-singh-saini`) — a NEW story block for era 10

**A living person, told only from an account he chose to make public: sworn testimony to a Senate
subcommittee, published by the Committee on the Judiciary.** Nothing below is inferred. **Source as in
§10i.**

- **Harpreet Singh Saini** was brought to the United States from India in 2004, aged ten, with his
  brother **Kamaljit Singh Saini**, by their parents.
- On **Sunday 5 August 2012** a white supremacist with a loaded gun walked into their gurdwara in **Oak
  Creek, Wisconsin** and killed his mother, **Paramjit Kaur**, while she was sitting for morning
  prayers, and five men, all of whom wore turbans as he does.
- **Two days later he and his brother ate the last rotis their mother had made from scratch the night
  before she died**, and understood that it was the last meal made by her hands they would ever eat.
- He had just started his first day of college, and his mother was not there to send him off.
- **He testified before a subcommittee of the United States Senate on 19 September 2012, forty-five days
  after she was killed.** His words: "I want to tell the gunman who took her from me: You may have been
  full of hate, but my mother was full of love."
- On his faith: "My mother was a devout Sikh. Like all Sikhs, she was bound to live in Chardi Kala — a
  state of high spirits and optimism. She was also taught as a Sikh to neither have fear of anyone nor
  strike fear in anyone."
- **What he asked Congress for: "I came here today to ask the government to give my mother the dignity
  of being a statistic. The FBI does not track hate crimes against Sikhs."**
- **Movie:** none verified. `movie=""`.
- **Slug checked against all 614 story slugs in `outlines/` — `harpreet-singh-saini` is unique.**

### 10k. Congregations dividing: The United Methodist Church, 2019 to 2024

**Source: the denomination's own publications.** UM News, "40-year ban on gay clergy struck down," by
Heather Hahn, **1 May 2024**, https://www.umnews.org/en/news/40-year-ban-on-gay-clergy-struck-down ;
and UMC.org, "Ask The UMC: Is The UMC really…? Part 5: Ending disaffiliation,"
https://www.umc.org/en/content/ask-the-umc-the-umc-really-is-part-5-ending-disaffiliation

- A special General Conference in **2019** added **paragraph 2553** to the Book of Discipline, letting a
  local congregation leave the denomination and keep its buildings for "reasons of conscience" over the
  decisions the 2019 conference had made about homosexuality. **The provision expired on 31 December
  2023.**
- **More than 7,600 United States congregations left under it — about 26 per cent of the denomination's
  United States congregations.**
- **On 1 May 2024, at the General Conference in Charlotte, North Carolina, delegates voted 692 to 51 to
  remove the ban on ordaining clergy who are "self-avowed practicing homosexuals," which had been in the
  Book of Discipline since 1984.** The same conference removed the ban on clergy and churches holding
  same-sex weddings, and deleted paragraph 2553.
- **How to write it: as what happened, in dates and votes. This chapter reports what a church decided
  and how many congregations left. It does not say who was right.**

### 10l. Worship on a screen

**Pew Research Center, "Online Religious Services Appeal to Many Americans, but Going in Person Remains
More Popular," survey of 11,377 U.S. adults conducted 16-27 November 2022, published 2 June 2023.**
https://www.pewresearch.org/wp-content/uploads/sites/20/2023/06/PF_2023.06.02_religion-online_REPORT.pdf

- **As of November 2022: 16 per cent of U.S. adults were regularly attending religious services only in
  person; 10 per cent were regularly watching only on a screen; 17 per cent were doing both. In total 43
  per cent were attending or watching at least monthly, and 57 per cent were doing neither.**
- **27 per cent of adults were watching services on screens**, counting the 10 per cent who only watched
  and the 17 per cent who did both.
- **Among those who did both, 76 per cent said they preferred going in person, 11 per cent preferred
  watching, and 14 per cent had no preference.**
- The most commonly given reason for watching on a screen was **convenience**, not fear of illness.
- **Label Pew as a polling organisation, give November 2022 as the date the answers were given, and give
  June 2023 as the date they were published.**

### 10m. The churches apologise for the boarding schools — already verified, in §7e

**Do not re-research this. All of it is sourced in §7e of this bank from the U.S. Department of the
Interior's *Federal Indian Boarding School Initiative Investigative Report, Volume II* (2024), pages
76-77.** In summary, and for the era-10 cell:

- **25 July 2022 — Pope Francis apologised in Canada** for the Church's part in Canada's residential
  schools: "I ask forgiveness, in particular, for the ways in which many members of the Church and of
  religious communities cooperated, not least through their indifference, in projects of cultural
  destruction and forced assimilation."
- **30 March 2023 — the Vatican renounced the Doctrine of Discovery.**
- **14 June 2024 — the United States Conference of Catholic Bishops voted 181 to 2** to adopt "Keeping
  Christ's Sacred Promise: A Pastoral Framework for Indigenous Ministry," a formal apology for the
  Catholic Church's part in the United States.
- **And the fact that must go with it:** the Department of the Interior records that it is "unaware of
  any support by the United States Conference of Catholic Bishops for His Holiness Pope Francis to
  officially apologize on behalf of the Holy Roman Catholic Church for its participation in U.S. Federal
  Indian boarding school policies."
- **`native-nations` LEADS on the schools themselves.** This chapter's part is which churches ran them
  (§7e: 210 of 417 federal schools had a religious affiliation) and what those churches have since said.


### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): abuse figures refreshed to 2026, and not only Catholic

**Why:** §10h stops at 2018 (grand jury) and CARA's 2020-22 decade. The perishable rule wants the newest
dated figures, and a reader should not come away thinking only one church hid abuse.

- **CARA re-checked 27 September 2026:** the spreadsheet at cara.georgetown.edu/faqs still has **2025**
  as its newest column (sisters 33,135; priests 33,462) and its abuse table still ends at **2020-22: 36**,
  with the next cell marked "TBA." **§10f and §10h stand as current.**
- **The bishops' own newest annual audit, released May 2026, covering 1 July 2024 to 30 June 2025:**
  **1,070 allegations reported by 973 victims-survivors**, across 194 Catholic dioceses and eparchies
  that reported; **168 more allegations than the year before**; **727 (68 per cent) came in through a
  lawyer**, and about 60 per cent arose from lawsuits, compensation programmes and bankruptcies. **Only
  24 allegations were made by people who were still children** (4 substantiated, 13 under investigation,
  7 unsubstantiated); most concerned abuse that happened decades ago. About 66 per cent of the accused
  were diocesan priests, and 54 per cent of the accused were dead by June 2025. **The dioceses reported
  $389.9 million in abuse-related costs in fiscal 2025, 61 per cent more than the year before**, which the
  report ties to settlements after states lifted time limits on lawsuits. (Gina Christian, OSV News,
  29 May 2026, via Angelus, https://angelusnews.com/news/nation/usccb-youth-protection-report-2026/ ;
  the 1,070 / 973 / 168 / 727 figures also appear in the USCCB's release as seen in a search summary.)
  **Label: the bishops' own audit, reported by a Catholic news service.**
- **The year before, for comparison:** the 2024 report (1 July 2023 to 30 June 2024), released **6 June
  2025**, counted **902 allegations by 855 victims-survivors.** (USCCB, "U.S. Bishops' Secretariat of
  Child and Youth Protection Releases Annual Report," 6 June 2025,
  https://www.usccb.org/news/2025/us-bishops-secretariat-child-and-youth-protection-releases-annual-report )
- **The Southern Baptist Convention, 2022.** Its Executive Committee commissioned an outside firm, **Guidepost
  Solutions**, whose report was published on **22 May 2022**. It found that survivors "were met, time and
  time again, with resistance, stonewalling, and even outright hostility," and that **D. August Boto**,
  the committee's former vice-president and general counsel, and **Roger Oldham**, a former SBC
  spokesman, both retired in 2019, had kept a private list of ministers accused of abuse. The committee
  released the list on **26 May 2022**: 205 pages, cases mainly from 2000 to 2019. (NBC News, 27 May 2022,
  journalism, https://www.nbcnews.com/news/us-news/southern-baptist-leaders-release-secret-list-accused-abusers-rcna30807 )
  **The list held 703 names** (unconfirmed: search summary only, WSOC-TV headline and Baptist press
  summaries); the report found some committee leaders "singularly focused on avoiding liability for the
  SBC" (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- **How to write it:** give the dated figures with whose figures they are. Do not add the numbers up
  across sources.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): Oak Creek corrected, and attacks on congregations 2017 to 2025

**CORRECTION NOTE to §10i (Oak Creek).** §10i, from Saini's 2012 testimony, says six were killed and that
"Santokh Singh and Punjab Singh were shot and survived." **A U.S. Senate resolution in 2022 counts seven
killed, and names Baba Punjab Singh as the seventh.** (Senator Tammy Baldwin, press release on the
Senate resolution for the tenth anniversary, 2 August 2022,
https://www.baldwin.senate.gov/news/press-releases/senator-baldwin-leads-senate-resolution-to-remember-the-10th-anniversary-of-shooting-at-the-sikh-temple-in-oak-creek )
He died of his wounds in 2020 (unconfirmed: search summary only). The same release names **Lieutenant
Brian Murphy**, "shot 15 times at close range," and Officer Savan Lenda. **The gunman was Wade Michael
Page, 40, a white supremacist, shot by a police officer and then killed himself** (unconfirmed: search
summary only; the Senate release does not name him). The outline was corrected by T-260c. Both counts
stand in the record: six killed on the day; seven by 2022.

- **Sutherland Springs, Texas, Sunday 5 November 2017.** At the **First Baptist Church**, **Devin Patrick
  Kelley** "entered the church and opened fire, killing twenty-six people and wounding twenty-two more,"
  then died by his own gunshot after fleeing. A federal court found that **the U.S. Air Force**, where
  Kelley had been convicted of a domestic-violence crime, failed to send his criminal record to the FBI,
  so a background check that should have stopped his gun purchases did not, and it held **the United
  States 60 per cent responsible.** The dead included Emily Hill, 11, and Megan Hill, 9 (the court's
  list of estates). (*Holcombe v. United States*, No. SA-18-CV-555-XR, W.D. Tex., findings on damages
  filed 7 February 2022, at 1 and the plaintiff list,
  https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCOURTS-txwd-5_18-cv-00555/pdf/USCOURTS-txwd-5_18-cv-00555-8.pdf ;
  the liability ruling is 2021 WL 2821125, 6 July 2021.) The Air Force detail beyond "failed to exercise
  reasonable care in its undertaking to submit Kelley's criminal history" is from a search summary.
- **Poway, California, Saturday 27 April 2019.** At the **Chabad of Poway**
  synagogue, **John T. Earnest** killed **Lori Gilbert-Kaye** and wounded three others, among them an
  eight-year-old child. He also admitted trying to burn down the **Dar-ul-Arqam mosque** in Escondido on
  24 March 2019. He pleaded guilty to a 113-count federal indictment (including 55 counts under the
  Church Arson Prevention Act of 1996) and was sentenced to **life plus 30 years**. (unconfirmed: search
  summary only, of the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of California and ATF releases;
  the justice.gov page rendered empty and atf.gov refused.)
- **Minneapolis, Wednesday 27 August 2025.** During an **all-school Mass** at the **Church of the
  Annunciation** in the first week of school, a shooter fired through the church windows at children in
  the pews. **Two children were killed; 26 children and 3 adults were injured by gunfire, and one more
  person was hurt in another way: 31 victims.** (City of Minneapolis, "Annunciation Remembrance Day,"
  page updated 26 August 2026,
  https://www.minneapolismn.gov/resident-services/public-safety/city-emergency/shooting-august-27/ ;
  "fired through the windows" and the shooter's death from a self-inflicted wound: NPR, Odette Yousef,
  29 August 2025, https://www.npr.org/2025/08/29/nx-s1-5522038/minneapolis-shooting-motive .) The
  shooter was **Robin Westman, 23** (NPR). The FBI called it an act of domestic terrorism driven by
  "hate-filled ideology" (NPR). **The children's names, Fletcher Merkel, 8, and Harper Moyski, 10:
  unconfirmed: search summary only.**
- **Grand Blanc Township, Michigan, Sunday 28 September 2025.** **Thomas Jacob Sanford, 40, of Burton,
  Michigan**, drove his pickup truck through the front doors of a meetinghouse of **the Church of Jesus
  Christ of Latter-day Saints** during Sunday services, opened fire and set the building on fire. **Four
  people were killed: Craig Hayden, Pat Howard, John Bond and Thelma Armstrong.** Nine others were
  wounded, aged 6 to 78. Police shot him dead within 3 minutes 43 seconds of the first 911 call. (WNEM-TV,
  30 December 2025, journalism,
  https://www.wnem.com/2025/12/30/look-back-tragic-church-attack-grand-blanc-township/ .) The FBI
  investigated it as "targeted violence" (search summary only). **No motive is settled in any source
  opened here. Do not give one.**
- **Charleston, the sentence.** A federal jury convicted Dylann Roof on 33 counts, including nine counts
  of obstructing religion resulting in death, and "unanimously recommended a death sentence on the
  religious-obstruction and firearm counts, and he was sentenced accordingly." The Fourth Circuit
  affirmed on 25 August 2021. (*United States v. Roof*, 10 F.4th 314, at the opening,
  https://www.ca4.uscourts.gov/opinions/173.P.pdf )
- **Colleyville, Texas, 15 January 2022** (a rabbi and three congregants held hostage at Congregation Beth
  Israel; all released or escaped; the hostage-taker killed) is known but **was not sourced here. Leave
  it out or research it.**

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): FBI hate-crime counts refreshed to the newest year (2025, published August 2026)

- **2025 data, released by the FBI in August 2026:** **2,408 religion-based hate-crime incidents**, of
  which **1,528 were anti-Jewish (63 per cent)**, down from **1,938 anti-Jewish incidents in 2024**. The
  FBI's data came from **16,791 law-enforcement agencies, 85.9 per cent of those enrolled.** (Anti-
  Defamation League press release, 17 August 2026, quoting the FBI data,
  https://www.adl.org/resources/press-release/2025-marked-third-highest-year-record-anti-jewish-hate-crimes-fbi-reports .)
  **Label: an advocacy group's reading of FBI figures.** One other Jewish group reported the share as
  69 per cent (search summary only). **Use 63 per cent with ADL named, or give the counts only.**
- **Anti-Sikh incidents: 177 in 2025, up from 142 in 2024, a 25 per cent rise.** (Sikh Coalition,
  18 August 2026, https://www.sikhcoalition.org/blog/2026/fbi-releases-2025-hate-crime-data-including-25-increase-in-anti-sikh-incidents/ .)
  A different figure, 226 single-bias incidents, appears in a search summary tied to the FBI's
  preliminary data of April 2026; **use the Sikh Coalition's August figure and say whose it is.**
- **Anti-Muslim incidents in 2025: 199** (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- **When the FBI began counting crimes against Sikhs:** the FBI agreed in 2013 and began collecting
  anti-Sikh, anti-Hindu and anti-Arab bias data in **2015** (unconfirmed: search summary only; ucr.fbi.gov
  and fbi.gov are behind a browser check). **This is the direct answer to Harpreet Singh Saini's 2012
  request.** Write the year only once a writer has opened a source for it.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): the classroom Ten Commandments cases, refreshed to September 2026, and the 2025 storybook case

**CORRECTION NOTE to §10e.** §10e says "As of 10 September 2026 the question of whether the law violates
the First Amendment had not been answered by any court." **That holds for Louisiana only. For Texas, the
full Fifth Circuit ruled on the merits question at the preliminary-injunction stage.**

- **Texas, Senate Bill 10.** In ***Rabbi Nathan v. Alamo Heights Independent School District***, the Fifth
  Circuit heard the case en banc from the start and, on **21 April 2026, by 9 votes to 8, "held that
  S.B. 10 does not violate either of the Religion Clauses,"** vacating a district court's preliminary
  injunction. The majority opinion, by **Judge Duncan**, held that *Stone v. Graham* (1980) "does not
  control" because it rested on the *Lemon* test that *Kennedy v. Bremerton* abandoned. (173 F.4th 576
  (5th Cir. 2026) (en banc), as described in the families' petition for certiorari, filed **17 August
  2026** by Americans United and others,
  https://www.au.org/wp-content/uploads/securepdfs/2026/08/Nathan-v.-Alamo-Heights-ISD-Cert-Petition-8.17.26.pdf ,
  at i and 14-15.) **Label: the ruling as the losing side summarises it; the vote and the holding are
  plain procedural facts in that document.** The petition's first question: "Whether the Establishment
  Clause forbids a state from mandating the permanent, conspicuous display of a government-selected
  version of the Ten Commandments in every public-school classroom."
- **Louisiana, H.B. 71.** Still as in §10e (en banc vacated the injunction on ripeness grounds, 20
  February 2026). A Louisiana teacher, **Chris Dier**, asked the Supreme Court to hear his challenge in
  August 2026 (unconfirmed: search summary only; Louisiana Illuminator, 25 August 2026).
- **As of 27 September 2026 the Supreme Court had not said whether it will hear either case** (no grant
  or denial found in the search; the petitions were filed in August and the Court's term opens in
  October). **Write it as undecided in the Supreme Court.**
- **The 2025 case about school books and parents' religion: *Mahmoud v. Taylor*, 606 U.S. 522, argued
  22 April 2025, decided 27 June 2025, 6 to 3.** In the 2022-23 school year the **Montgomery County,
  Maryland** school board added five "LGBTQ+-inclusive" storybooks for kindergarten to fifth grade, first
  let parents excuse their children, then withdrew the opt-out. The parents who sued "come from diverse
  religious backgrounds": **Tamer Mahmoud and Enas Barakat, who are Muslims; Jeff Roman, a Catholic, and
  Svitlana Roman, Ukrainian Orthodox; and Chris and Melissa Persak, Catholics**, with a parents' group,
  Kids First. **Held (Justice Alito, joined by Roberts, Thomas, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh and Barrett): the
  parents were entitled to a preliminary injunction letting their children be excused**, relying on
  *Wisconsin v. Yoder*: parents have a right "to direct the religious upbringing of their children."
  **Justice Sotomayor, dissenting with Kagan and Jackson:** public schools "offer to children of all faiths
  and backgrounds an education and an opportunity to practice living in our multicultural society. ...
  Yet it will become a mere memory if children must be insulated from exposure to ideas and concepts that
  may conflict with their parents' religious beliefs." (Slip opinion, syllabus and at 1-2 of the
  dissent, https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/24pdf/24-297_4f14.pdf .) **This is the Yoder line of
  era 9 carried into 2025, and no other chapter's bank has it.** Parked for `education` and
  `government-politics`.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-260c): Oak Flat, 2014 to 2026, a sacred place transferred (land, brief item 5)

- **Oak Flat, Chí'chil Biłdagoteel in Western Apache**, in the Tonto National Forest, Arizona, is used for
  ceremonies including the **Sunrise Ceremony** and was listed on the National Register of Historic Places
  in 2016. Congress ordered it traded to **Resolution Copper**, a company owned by **Rio Tinto and BHP**,
  in the **Southeast Arizona Land Exchange and Conservation Act of 2014**, attached to the annual defence
  bill. **The exchange of about 2,400 acres was completed in March 2026**, and the land is now private.
  The company expects its mine to leave a crater about two miles wide within about four decades.
  (Gabriel Pietrorazio, KJZZ, 23 March 2026, journalism,
  https://www.kjzz.org/tribal-natural-resources/2026-03-23/whats-next-for-oak-flat-the-apache-holy-land-that-now-belongs-to-resolution-copper .)
  The Supreme Court declined to hear **Apache Stronghold v. United States** in May 2025, and Apache
  Stronghold filed a new suit in April 2026 (unconfirmed: search summary only, Becket and KJZZ 15 April
  2026).
- **Why it belongs here:** it is the *Lyng* question of 1988 again (era 9 PATCH above), a sacred place on
  federal land and whether the religion clause protects it. `native-nations` may lead on the land.

  fetched with curl 2026-09-30): religious-bias offences in 2015 were 1,354; "51.3 percent were

- Press release of Representatives Joe Crowley, John Garamendi, Ami Bera, Bill Pascrell, Mike Honda,
  Grace Meng and Judy Chu (garamendi.house.gov, "After 3 Years and Tragic Massacre in Wisconsin, House
  Members, Advocates Welcome Federal System...", fetched 2026-09-30; the page's date stamp reads 9 November
  2022, but the text says the change came "Two years later" than the March 2013 letter, and the
  Representatives named were in office in 2015): after the March 2013 letter from more than 100 members of
  Congress, the FBI's Advisory Policy Board recommended collecting the data, and the Justice Department
  and FBI updated the hate-crime database and training manual "to start tracking hate crimes committed

### PATCH 2026-09-30 (T-327b): Damon Landor, 2020 to 2026 (copied from `styles`)
Copied with its source from `research/research-styles.md` (T-272c, era 10).
- In 2020 guards at Raymond Laborde Correctional Center in Louisiana threw away a court decision he showed
  them, handcuffed him to a chair and shaved off his dreadlocks, which he had grown for decades under a

### PATCH 2026-10-02 (T-485): Sutherland Springs, the background checks, from the 6 July 2021 liability findings
- *Holcombe v. United States*, No. SA-18-CV-555-XR (W.D. Tex.), ECF No. 452, "Findings of Fact and Conclusions
  of Law," signed 6 July 2021 by **Judge Xavier Rodriguez**
  (https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCOURTS-txwd-5_18-cv-00555/pdf/USCOURTS-txwd-5_18-cv-00555-6.pdf,
  downloaded and read with pdftotext 2026-10-02):
  - Finding 2: in four gun purchases from licensed dealers, "the retailer ran the mandatory background check
    through the National Instant Criminal Background Check System ('NICS') administered by the FBI. In each
    instance, the response from the NICS was that the retailer could proceed with the sale."
  - Finding 3: "Kelley should not have cleared the background check, however, because he had been convicted
    of a disqualifying offense in November 2012 while he was serving in the United States Air Force," at
    Holloman Air Force Base, New Mexico. Finding 4: a general court-martial convicted him of assaulting his
    then-wife, Tessa Brennaman, and his stepson.
  - Finding 41: Air Force Office of Special Investigations agents took his fingerprints on 9 June 2011 "but
    did not mail them to the FBI." Finding 95: after his conviction, confinement-facility staff "failed to
    collect or submit Kelley's fingerprints for inclusion in the NICS."
  - Finding 27: a reported felony assault conviction "results in an automatic denial."
  - "It is undisputed that every firearm in Kelley's possession ... was purchased from an FFL [licensed
    dealer] after he passed a NICS background check."
  - Apportionment: "had the Government done its job and properly reported Kelley's information into the
    background check system--it is more likely than not that Kelley would have been deterred from carrying
    out the Church shooting." Conclusion: the United States "60% responsible."
- Confirms the search-summary line above.
- **Louisiana H.B. 71 signed 19 June 2024.** "Louisiana Republican Gov. Jeff Landry signed a bill, H.B. 71,
  on Wednesday requiring all public schools in the state to display a poster of the Ten Commandments in
  each classroom." (Reason, 20 June 2024, https://reason.com/2024/06/20/thou-shalt-not-covet-state-power/ ,
  journalism, WebFetch 2026-10-02; Wednesday = 19 June 2024.)
- **The Doctrine of Discovery, defined.** Catholic News Service via USCCB, "Responding to Indigenous,
  Vatican disavows 'doctrine of discovery'," 30 March 2023,
  https://www.usccb.org/news/2023/responding-indigenous-vatican-disavows-doctrine-discovery (WebFetch
  2026-10-02): "a collection of papal texts ... that appeared to bless the efforts of explorers to colonize
  and claim the lands of any people who were not Christian"; the Vatican said it "is not part of the
  teaching of the Catholic Church."

## Parked material from other chapters (kept from the pre-research file)

## Parked from `native-nations` (2026-08-07)
- **The Ghost Dance as a religious movement:** Wovoka, a Northern Paiute man from the Walker Lake country of Nevada, had a vision during the solar eclipse of **January 1, 1889** — ethical living, peace, and a round dance that would reunite the living with the dead and restore the world. The religion spread across the Plains reservations in 1889–90; fear of it (stoked by agents and newspapers) led to Sitting Bull's killing (Dec 15, 1890) and the Wounded Knee massacre (Dec 29, 1890). `native-nations` leads on the events; religion can treat the belief itself — a revitalization religion born in catastrophe. (Sources: Britannica "Wovoka"; EBSCO "The Messiah Letter"; PBS American Experience "The Lakota Ghost Dance.")
- **The Pueblo Revolt (August 10, 1680) as a revolt against forced conversion:** led by a religious leader (Po'pay, Tewa, Ohkay Owingeh) after decades of suppression of Pueblo ceremony; 21 Franciscan friars were killed and mission churches burned across the province; Spain was out for 12 years. Religion's angle: missions and forced conversion in the Southwest; `native-nations` leads on the revolt as politics. (Sources: Britannica "Pueblo Rebellion"; EBSCO; Indian Pueblo Cultural Center.)
- **Boarding schools and the churches:** many federal Indian boarding schools were run or supported by religious institutions (stated in the coverage of the 2024 federal report and the presidential apology — e.g., Washington Post/CBS, Oct 25, 2024). When this chapter is worked, verify the denominational breakdown from DOI Investigative Report Vol. 2 directly. Ruling 7: religion covers religious instruction in schools; native-nations leads on the boarding-school system.

## Parked from `storytelling-evolution` (2026-09-27, T-269)

- `native-nations` and `religion`, era 07 (1850-1900): Moravian missionaries arrived at Quinhagak, Alaska, in 1885, called Yup'ik masked dancing "heathen rites" and "idol worship," and recruited Native helpers as missionaries; no traditional dancing took place in Quinhagak for over a century, until 2013. Source: *Archaeology* magazine, Sept/Oct 2015, https://archaeology.org/issues/187-1509/features/3558-alaska-yupik-cultural-revival . (This chapter's T-269b should also use it.)
- `religion`, era 03 (1600s), if not already present: Franciscan custodian Alonso de Posada forbade Pueblo ceremonies 1656-1665 and ordered missionaries into kivas to burn masks and take sacred objects, under soldiers' protection; 1675 Governor Treviño's arrest of 47 religious leaders (3 hanged, 1 suicide, rest whipped and jailed). Source: Dennis Zotigh, NMAI, Smithsonian Voices, https://www.smithsonianmag.com/blogs/national-museum-american-indian/2026/09/02/pueblo-natives-southwest-launched-first-successful-shortlived-american-revolution/ . (`holidays` bank already carries the same facts.)
